Gauge invariant variational formulations of electromagnetic gyrokinetic theory
Ronald Remmerswaal, Roman Hatzky, Eric Sonnendrücker

Abstract
The use of gyrokinetics, wherein phase-space coordinate transformations result in a phase-space dimensionality reduction as well as removal of fast time scales, has enabled the simulation of microturbulence in fusion devices. The state-of-the-art gyrokinetic models used in practice are parallel-only models wherein the perpendicular part of the vector potential is neglected. Such models are inherently not gauge invariant. We generalize the work of [Burby, Brizard. Physics Letters A , 383(18):2172–2175] by deriving a sufficient condition on the gyrocentre coordinate transformation which ensures gauge invariance. This leads to a parametrized family of gyrokinetic models for which we motivate a specific choice of parameters that results in a physically meaningful gauge invariant model. Due to gauge invariance this model can be expressed directly in terms of the electromagnetic fields, rather than the potentials, and the gyrokinetic model thereby results in the macroscopic Maxwell’s equations. For the linearized model, it is demonstrated that the shear and compressional Alfvén waves are present with the correct frequencies. The fast compressional Alfvén wave can be removed by making use of a Darwin-like approximation. This approximation breaks gauge invariance, but we find that the field equations are still compatible without the need of a Lagrange multiplier.



1
Introduction

The role of numerical modelling is prevalent not only in understanding the physics of fusion plasmas, but also in the design and optimization of magnetic fusion devices.
The collisionless Vlasov–Maxwell model is found to be appropriate when the collision frequency of the charged particles is much lower than the frequencies that are of interest, e.g. when studying microturbulence
(
Garbet et al. 2010
)
.
Nonetheless, such a model is still very challenging to use, not only because of its six dimensional phase-space, but also because of the large range of length (four orders of magnitude between the plasma size and the Debye length) and time (seven orders of magnitude between the ion-ion collision frequency and the electron plasma frequency) scales.

Two of the aforementioned challenges are, at least partially, addressed by gyrokinetic theory
(
Frieman & Chen 1982
;
Littlejohn 1983
;
Sugama 2000
;
Brizard & Hahm 2007
;
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
wherein a sequence of phase-space coordinate transformations is used to decouple the fast gyration of a charged particle from its otherwise slower motion along the magnetic field.
This thereby results in the removal of the (high) cyclotron frequency, while at the same time reducing the phase-space dimensionality by one.
When moreover considering the quasi-neutral limit, it is found that also the Langmuir wave (or plasma oscillation) is removed from the model.
The use of gyrokinetic theory thereby permits the numerical modelling of turbulent transport in tokamaks, as discussed in the well written review paper
(
Garbet et al. 2010
)
and was more recently applied to study electromagnetic turbulence in stellarator plasmas
(
Mishchenko et al. 2023
)
.

The gyrokinetic model results from a sequence of – mostly near-identity – phase-space coordinate transformations which are applied to the collisionless Vlasov–Maxwell model, and therefore
in theory
there is no approximation error.
In practice, however, one must always truncate the near-identity phase-space coordinate transformation to some order of the small expansion parameter, resulting in an unavoidable modelling error.
The more recently developed gyrokinetic models are based on a variational principle, which thereby, despite this modelling error, still preserve essential structures of the original Vlasov–Maxwell model.
For instance the total (free) charge, momentum and energy should be conserved, while the choice of gauge condition on the vector potential should leave the model invariant, resulting in so-called gauge invariance.

However, to our knowledge all global gyrokinetic simulations either neglect the part of the vector potential that is perpendicular to the background magnetic field and thereby result in a ‘parallel-only’ gyrokinetic model
(
Kleiber et al. 2016
)
, or use a simplified model for the parallel component of the perturbed magnetic field
(
Chen & Zonca 2016
)
.
Both approximations are irreconcilable with gauge invariance.
Moreover, it is unclear which physics is neglected in such approximations.
For these reasons we extend the work of
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
and propose a family of gyrokinetic models which are expressed in terms of the full vector potential and are gauge invariant by construction.
We have presented the derivation of the gyrokinetic model in an easily accessible way, without requiring any knowledge of differential geometry or Lie transforms.
Moreover, our final model results in equations of motion as well as field equations which have physically meaningful terms, reduce to well-known parallel-only models from the literature
(
Kleiber et al. 2016
)
, and are suitable for discretization.

Our paper is structured as follows.
In
Section

2
we discuss the preliminary phase-space coordinate transformation leading to the single-particle guiding-centre phase-space Lagrangian, wherein only the stationary background magnetic field is considered.
The perturbed time-dependent electromagnetic fields are included as a perturbation to the guiding-centre Lagrangian in
Section

3
, followed by a detailed description of the proposed gyrocentre coordinate transformation, resulting eventually in the single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian.
We then combine the single-particle Lagrangian for each species with Maxwell’s equations in
Section

4
, eventually resulting in the gyrocentre equations of motion, the quasi-neutrality equation as well as Ampère’s law.
A comparison with reduced models is made in
Section

5
, where we not only compare our model to the parallel-only model, but also propose a Darwin-like approximation of our gyrokinetic model to eliminate the compressional Alfvén wave.
We conclude with a discussion in
Section

6
.

2
Preliminary transformations

In this section we establish most of our notation and apply preliminary coordinate transformations which result in the single-particle guiding-centre phase-space Lagrangian wherein only a background magnetic field is considered.

2.1
Motivation

We start by considering the model for the motion of a charged particle, of charge
q
and mass
m
, in the presence of a stationary background magnetic field
{\bm{B}}_{0}=\nabla\times{\bm{A}}_{0}
, with magnitude
B_{0}=\lvert{\bm{B}}_{0}\rvert
, where
{\bm{A}}_{0}
denotes the background vector potential and
\lvert\cdot\rvert
denotes the Euclidean norm.
The background magnetic field yields a coordinate system whose orthogonal basis vectors are denoted by
({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0},{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1},{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2})
, where
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}={\bm{B}}_{0}/B_{0}
, and
{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1},{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}
are unit vectors orthogonal to
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}
for which
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}={\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}
.
For any vector
{\bm{S}}
(such as the velocity
{\bm{U}}
), we denote the component parallel to the background magnetic field as

S_{\shortparallel}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{S}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}.

(2.1)

The resulting parallel and perpendicular parts of a vector are denoted by

{\bm{S}}_{\shortparallel}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=S_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0},\quad{\bm{S}}_{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{S}}-{\bm{S}}_{\shortparallel}.

(2.2)

Equivalent notation is used for the perpendicular gradient operator:
\nabla_{\perp}Q\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nabla Q-(\nabla Q\bcdot{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}){\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}
.

It is well-known that the following single-particle phase-space Lagrangian
L_{0}
is a model for the motion of a charged particle in physical coordinates

L_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Gamma_{0}-H_{0},\quad\Gamma_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(q{\bm{A}}_{0}+m{\bm{U}}\right)\bcdot\dot{{\bm{R}}},\quad H_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{m}{2}\lvert{\bm{U}}\rvert^{2},

(2.3)

where
\Gamma_{0}
and
H_{0}
denote the symplectic and Hamiltonian part of the Lagrangian
L_{0}
, respectively.
The model is expressed in terms of the phase-space coordinates
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=({\bm{R}},{\bm{U}})\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
, where
{\bm{R}}
and
{\bm{U}}
denote the particle position and velocity, respectively.

Imposing the principle of least action (this will be explained in more detail in
Section

2.5
) on the single-particle Lagrangian (
2.3
) results in the well-known Euler–Lagrange equations, which in turn yield the equations of motion (EOMs) for a charged particle in the presence of a stationary background magnetic field

\dot{{\bm{R}}}={\bm{U}},\quad\dot{{\bm{U}}}=\frac{q}{m}{\bm{U}}\times{\bm{B}}_{0}.

(2.4)

If
{\bm{B}}_{0}
is constant then it can easily be verified that the following is the solution to the EOMs

{\bm{U}}(t)=U_{\shortparallel}(0){\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}+\left[\cos(\omega_{\mathrm{c}}t)({\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}+{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}})+\sin(\omega_{\mathrm{c}}t)({\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}})\right]{\bm{U}}_{\perp}(0),

(2.5)

where we have defined the cyclotron frequency as

\omega_{\mathrm{c}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{qB_{0}}{m}.

(2.6)

In many applications the frequency of interest is much smaller than the cyclotron frequency and therefore the aim is to decouple this fast gyrating motion by applying coordinate transformations to the single-particle phase-space Lagrangian.

2.2
Field aligned velocity coordinates

Figure 1
:
Illustration of the guiding-centre coordinate system.
We denote the physical particle position in black and the guiding-centre position in green.
The particle moves along the background magnetic field in the (blue)
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}
direction, while gyrating in the (red) plane perpendicular to the background magnetic field, in the direction of the (red) arrow
{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}
.
The extremal values of the
\varsigma
parameter (introduced in
Section

3.4
) are indicated in grey.

The first coordinate transformation that we consider results in coordinates which are field aligned in velocity space
{\bm{Z}}=({\bm{R}},U_{\shortparallel},M,\Theta)
, where the parallel velocity component
U_{\shortparallel}
, magnetic moment
M
, and gyro-phase
\Theta
are defined as

U_{\shortparallel}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{U}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0},\quad M\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{mU_{\tau}^{2}}{2B_{0}},\quad\Theta\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\arctan\left(\frac{{\bm{U}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}}{{\bm{U}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}}\right),

(2.7)

where
U_{\tau}
is defined below.
Using the gyro-phase we define the new coordinate system
({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0},{\bm{\hat{\tau}}},{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})
, where
{\bm{\hat{\tau}}},{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}
are given by

{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}\sin\Theta-{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}\cos\Theta,\quad{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}\cos\Theta-{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}\sin\Theta

(2.8)

and are such that
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}={\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}
.
See also
Fig.

1
.
We denote the tangential and radial components of a vector field
{\bm{S}}
by

S_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{S}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\tau}}},\quad S_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{S}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}.

(2.9)

Note that

\tan\Theta=\frac{{\bm{U}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}}{{\bm{U}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}}=\frac{U_{\tau}\tan\Theta-U_{\rho}}{U_{\tau}+U_{\rho}\tan\Theta}\quad\implies\quad U_{\rho}=0

(2.10)

and therefore the velocity can be expressed in terms of the field aligned velocity coordinates as

{\bm{U}}=U_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}+U_{\tau}{\bm{\hat{\tau}}},

(2.11)

where the signed tangential velocity can be obtained from the magnetic moment as follows

U_{\tau}=\sign(q)\sqrt{\frac{2MB_{0}}{m}}.

(2.12)

The single-particle phase-space Lagrangian in field aligned velocity coordinates is now expressed as

L_{0}={\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\bcdot\dot{{\bm{Z}}}-H_{0},\quad{\bm{\gamma}}_{0,{\bm{R}}}=q{\bm{A}}_{0}+mU_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}+mU_{\tau}{\bm{\hat{\tau}}},\quad H_{0}=\frac{mU_{\shortparallel}^{2}}{2}+MB_{0},

(2.13)

where the remaining components of
{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}
are zero.
Note that the symplectic part of the Lagrangian is now written as
{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\bcdot\dot{{\bm{Z}}}
, where we interchangeably refer to
{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}
as the symplectic part of the Lagrangian.

2.3
Small parameters

Before we discuss near identity phase-space coordinate transformations, we discuss the corresponding small parameters in which the coordinate transformation is expanded.

We let
L_{B}
be the length scale on which the background magnetic field varies and let
\varrho
denote the Larmor radius

L_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{[B_{0}]}{[\nabla B_{0}]},\quad\varrho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{mu_{\mathrm{th}}}{q[B_{0}]},\quad u_{\mathrm{th}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{\frac{2k_{\mathrm{B}}T}{m}},

(2.14)

where
[Q]
is the constant dimensional part of
Q
and
u_{\mathrm{th}},k_{\mathrm{B}},T
denote the thermal velocity, the Boltzmann constant and the temperature, respectively.
The ratio of the two length scales is denoted by
\varepsilon_{B}

\varepsilon_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\varrho}{L_{B}},

(2.15)

which is much smaller than one when the background magnetic field has a weak inhomogeneity
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
.
This is the parameter that is used in the guiding-centre coordinate transformation.

We let
\varepsilon_{\delta}
denote the size of the perturbed magnetic field, which will be introduced in
Section

3
, relative to the background magnetic field,
and we use the first subscript of any function or vector field to indicate the magnitude in terms of
\varepsilon_{\delta}
:

\varepsilon_{\delta}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{[{\bm{B}}_{1}]}{[{\bm{B}}_{0}]},\quad Q_{l}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}^{l}).

(2.16)

It is assumed that the perturbed electric field
{\bm{E}}_{1}
scales identically, such that any function linear in
{\bm{E}}_{1}
is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta})
.
This is the parameter that is used in the gyrocentre coordinate transformation, which is discussed in
Section

3
.
The smallness of this parameter is motivated in
Section

4.5
.

Frequencies are nondimensionalized using the cyclotron frequency resulting in the nondimensional frequency
\varepsilon_{\omega}

\varepsilon_{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\omega}{[\omega_{\mathrm{c}}]},

(2.17)

which is a small parameter in the magnetic fusion devices that we consider
(
Zoni & Possanner 2021
)
.
The assumed smallness of this parameter plays a crucial role in the approximation of the perturbed gyrocentre Lagrangian, which is discussed in
Section

3.5.2
.

Finally, we nondimensionalize the perpendicular length scale
2\pi/k_{\perp}
(that is, the typical length scale in the plane perpendicular to
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}
) by the Larmor radius, which results in the nondimensional wavenumber
\varepsilon_{\perp}

\varepsilon_{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=k_{\perp}\varrho.

(2.18)

We emphasize that this last parameter is not necessarily small; in particular when turbulence is considered we find that
\varepsilon_{\perp}\sim 1
.
This parameter is used to approximate the second-order (in
\varepsilon_{\delta}
) gyrocentre Hamiltonian in
Section

3.5.3
.

2.4
Guiding-centre coordinates

The second coordinate transformation that we consider results in the guiding-centre coordinates
\bar{{\bm{Z}}}=(\bar{{\bm{R}}},\bar{U}_{\shortparallel},\bar{M},\bar{\Theta})
.
This transformation is aimed specifically at removing the gyro-phase dependence of
{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}
(which depends on the gyro-phase via the coordinate vector
{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}({\bm{R}},\Theta)
).
It results in the desired decoupling of the EOM for
\Theta
from the other EOMs and thereby also decouples the fast gyrating motion of the particle.

The leading-order (in
\varepsilon_{B}
) contribution to the near identity coordinate transformation of the particle position is given by (see
Fig.

1
)

\displaystyle\bar{{\bm{R}}}={\bm{R}}-{\bm{\rho}},

(2.19)

where the particle radial vector and gyroradius are defined as

{\bm{\rho}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\rho{\bm{\hat{\rho}}},\quad\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{m\bar{U}_{\tau}}{qB_{0}},

(2.20)

and we note that a derivation of this well-known result can be found in
(
Brizard 1990
, Eq. (2.58))
.
The transformation of the remaining phase-space coordinates is not of interest to us here and therefore we do not list them.
The resulting guiding-centre single-particle phase-space Lagrangian is given by
(
Brizard 1990
, Eq. (2.57))

\bar{L}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\bar{H}_{0},\quad\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0,{\bm{R}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q{\bm{A}}_{0}^{\star},\quad\bar{\gamma}_{0,\Theta}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{m\bar{M}}{q},\quad\bar{H}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{m\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}^{2}}{2}+\bar{M}B_{0},

(2.21)

where the effective guiding-centre vector potential is defined as
1
1

1

In
(
Brizard 1990
, Eq. (2.57))

{\bm{w}}_{0}
is denoted by
{\bm{W}}
, and we note that we neglect the
\frac{1}{2}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot\nabla\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})
term in
{\bm{W}}
as this term is not important for obtaining gyro-gauge invariance.

{\bm{A}}_{0}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{A}}_{0}+\frac{m\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}{q}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}-\frac{m\bar{M}}{q^{2}}{\bm{w}}_{0},\quad{\bm{w}}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\nabla{\bm{\hat{\tau}}})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}.

(2.22)

The relevance of the
{\bm{w}}_{0}
term becomes apparent when considering the transformation

\Theta\mapsto\Theta+\Psi(\bar{{\bm{R}}}),\quad{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{i}\mapsto\mathsfbi{T}(-\Psi){\bm{\hat{e}}}_{i},

(2.23)

where the rotation matrix
\mathsfbi{T}
is such that

\mathsfbi{T}(\psi){\bm{\hat{\tau}}}({\bm{R}},\Theta)={\bm{\hat{\tau}}}({\bm{R}},\Theta+\psi),\quad\mathsfbi{T}(\psi){\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}={\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}.

(2.24)

Invariance of the Lagrangian under this transformation reflects that we should be free to choose our coordinate vectors
{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{i}
. This is referred to as gyro-gauge invariance.
Indeed, we find that two terms of the symplectic part of the Lagrangian now depend on the gyro-phase
\bar{\Theta}
or the coordinate vectors
{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{i}
, and their sum is given by

-{\bm{w}}_{0}\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\dot{\bar{\Theta}}=\left(-\nabla{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\dot{\bar{\Theta}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\right)\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}=-\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\rho}}},

(2.25)

which is invariant under the transformation given by
Eq.

2.23
.

Note that we can furthermore show that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta}\left[(\nabla{\bm{\hat{\tau}}})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\right]=(\nabla{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}-(\nabla{\bm{\hat{\tau}}})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla({\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla({\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\tau}}})={\bm{0}}_{3},

(2.26)

from which it follows that
{\bm{w}}_{0}
, and therefore also
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}
is gyro-phase independent.
This implies that we can select the value
\Theta=\pi/2
resulting in

{\bm{w}}_{0}=\left.\left[(\nabla{\bm{\hat{\tau}}})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\right]\right|_{\Theta=\pi/2}=(\nabla{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}.

(2.27)

2.5
Principle of least action

Provided with the single-particle guiding-centre phase-space Lagrangian, we impose the principle of least action to obtain the corresponding EOMs.
That is, we impose

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}\int_{t^{0}}^{t^{1}}\bar{L}_{0}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}}+\varepsilon{\bm{\delta}},\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}+\varepsilon\dot{{\bm{\delta}}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=0,

(2.28)

where
{\bm{\delta}}
is arbitrary with
{\bm{\delta}}(t^{0})={\bm{\delta}}(t^{1})={\bm{0}}_{6}
.
This results in the well-known Euler–Lagrange equations, which are given by

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial\bar{L}_{0}}{\partial\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\frac{\partial\bar{L}_{0}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\quad\iff\quad\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}=\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\left(\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\right),

(2.29)

where we have defined the Lagrange and Poisson matrices as

\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}},\quad\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0})^{-1},

(2.30)

respectively.
Here the components of the Jacobian matrix are given by

\left(\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\right)_{ij}=\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0,i}}{\partial\bar{Z}_{j}}

(2.31)

and
{}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}
denotes the transpose of a matrix.

Provided with the Poisson matrix
\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}
, we can define the guiding-centre Poisson bracket as

\{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\bcdot\left(\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\right),

(2.32)

which allows the EOMs as given by
Eq.

2.29
to be expressed as

\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}=\{\bar{{\bm{Z}}},\bar{H}_{0}\}_{0},

(2.33)

where we have made use of the time-independence of
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}
, and we evaluate the bracket component-wise:
(\{\bar{{\bm{Z}}},\bar{H}_{0}\}_{0})_{i}=\{\bar{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{0}\}_{0}
.

When using our expression for the symplectic part of the Lagrangian
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}
, as given by
Eq.

2.21
, we find that the Lagrange matrix is given by

\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}_{0}^{\star}&-m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}&\frac{m}{q}{\bm{w}}_{0}&{\bm{0}}_{3}\\
m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&0\\
-\frac{m}{q}{\bm{w}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&\frac{m}{q}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&-\frac{m}{q}&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(2.34)

where we have defined the matrix

\mathsfbi{B}_{0}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\nabla{\bm{A}}_{0}^{\star})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\nabla{\bm{A}}_{0}^{\star}

(2.35)

for which

\mathsfbi{B}_{0}^{\star}{\bm{S}}={\bm{S}}\times{\bm{B}}_{0}^{\star},\quad{\bm{B}}_{0}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nabla\times{\bm{A}}_{0}^{\star}.

(2.36)

Inversion of the Lagrange matrix, resulting in the Poisson matrix, is somewhat tedious and is therefore described in detail in
Appendix

A
.
The result is given by

\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathsfbi{B}_{0}}{qB_{0}B_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}&\frac{{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}}{m}&{\bm{0}}_{3}&-\frac{{\bm{w}}_{0}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}}{qB_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}\\
-\frac{({\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{m}&0&0&-\frac{{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}\bcdot{\bm{w}}_{0}}{m}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&-\frac{q}{m}\\
\frac{({\bm{w}}_{0}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{qB_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}&\frac{{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}\bcdot{\bm{w}}_{0}}{m}&\frac{q}{m}&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(2.37)

where we have defined

{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{{\bm{B}}_{0}^{\star}}{B_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}={\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}+\frac{1}{qB_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}\left[m\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{m\bar{M}}{q}(\nabla\times{\bm{w}}_{0})_{\perp}\right]

(2.38)

and the curvature vector
{\bm{\kappa}}
:

{\bm{\kappa}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\nabla\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}.

(2.39)

The matrix
\mathsfbi{B}_{0}
is defined analogously to
Eq.

2.36
and therefore is given by

\mathsfbi{B}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\nabla{\bm{A}}_{0})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\nabla{\bm{A}}_{0}\quad\implies\quad\mathsfbi{B}_{0}{\bm{S}}={\bm{S}}\times{\bm{B}}_{0}.

(2.40)

This results in the following guiding-centre Poisson bracket

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\}_{0}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}}{qB_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}\bcdot(\nabla\mathcal{F}\times\nabla\mathcal{G})+\frac{{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}}{m}\bcdot\left(\nabla\mathcal{F}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}-\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}\nabla\mathcal{G}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{q}{m}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{M}}-\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{M}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}\right)+\frac{{\bm{w}}_{0}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}}{qB_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}\bcdot\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}\nabla\mathcal{G}-\nabla\mathcal{F}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}\bcdot{\bm{w}}_{0}}{m}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}-\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}\right)

(2.41)

by substituting
Eq.

2.37
into
Eq.

2.32
.
Substitution of
Sections

2.5
and
2.21
in
Eq.

2.32
yields the following guiding-centre EOMs

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}

\displaystyle=\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}+\frac{\bar{M}}{qB_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\nabla B_{0},

(2.42a)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\bar{M}}{m}{\bm{b}}_{0}^{\star}\bcdot\nabla B_{0},

(2.42b)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{M}}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.42c)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\Theta}}

\displaystyle=\omega_{\mathrm{c}}+{\bm{w}}_{0}\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}.

(2.42d)

Note that whereas the guiding-centre EOMs still contain the fast gyrating motion for which the frequency is given by the cyclotron frequency
\omega_{\mathrm{c}}
, this motion has been decoupled from the EOMs for the guiding-centre position and parallel velocity.
This means that if one is not interested in the gyro-phase
\bar{\Theta}
, then the corresponding EOM can be omitted entirely, thereby resulting in a phase-space dimensionality reduction.

2.6
Discussion on guiding-centre coordinates

We now compare the guiding-centre EOMs given by
Eq.

2.42
to the EOMs in physical coordinates as given by
Eq.

2.4
.
When integrating
Eq.

2.5
in time, we find that the physical particle position is given by

\displaystyle{\bm{R}}(t)={}

\displaystyle{\bm{R}}(0)+U_{\shortparallel}(0){\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}\left[\sin(\omega_{\mathrm{c}}t)({\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}+{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}})-(\cos(\omega_{\mathrm{c}}t)-1)({\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}})\right]{\bm{U}}_{\perp}(0),

(2.43)

where we recall that this result holds only if
{\bm{B}}_{0}
is constant.
Under the same assumption, we find that the guiding-centre EOMs result in

\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}=\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0},\quad\dot{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}=0,\quad\dot{\bar{M}}=0,\quad\dot{\bar{\Theta}}=\omega_{\mathrm{c}},

(2.44)

which upon integration in time yields

\bar{{\bm{R}}}(t)=\bar{{\bm{R}}}(0)+\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}(0){\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}t,\quad\bar{M}(t)=\bar{M}(0),\quad\bar{\Theta}(t)=\bar{\Theta}(0)+\omega_{\mathrm{c}}t.

(2.45)

According to
(
Brizard 1990
, Eq. (2.58))
the velocity coordinates
(U_{\shortparallel},M,\Theta)
transform trivially under the guiding-centre coordinate transformation whenever
{\bm{B}}_{0}
is constant and therefore
Eq.

2.45
can be written in physical coordinates as

{\bm{R}}(t)={\bm{R}}(0)+U_{\shortparallel}(0){\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}t+\frac{1}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}\left[{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}(\Theta^{0}+\omega_{\mathrm{c}}t)-{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}(\Theta^{0})\right]\bar{U}_{\tau}(0),

(2.46)

upon substitution of
Eqs.

2.19
,
2.20
and
2.6
and letting
\Theta(0)=\Theta^{0}
.
Here we use the notational convention, as we do throughout this paper, that a superscripted
0
indicates the initial value.

By making use of
{\bm{U}}_{\perp}(0)=U_{\tau}(0){\bm{\hat{\tau}}}(\Theta^{0})
, which follows from
Eq.

2.11
, it can be shown that the solutions given by
Sections

2.6
and
2.46
are identical, thereby confirming that we have consistently decoupled the fast gyrating motion using the guiding-centre coordinate transformation.

3
Single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian

Thus far, we have discussed a model for the motion of a charged particle in the presence of a stationary background magnetic field
{\bm{B}}_{0}
, where the introduction of the guiding-centre coordinates has resulted in decoupling the fast gyration and has furthermore resulted in a phase-space dimensionality reduction.
However, the moving charged particle itself deposits a charge and current and thereby generates an electromagnetic field, which in turn affects the motion of the particle.
In this section we introduce a ‘perturbation’ to the single-particle guiding-centre phase-space Lagrangian in the form of time-varying electromagnetic potentials, which in
Section

4
allows us to derive a self-consistent formulation of our gyrokinetic model.

3.1
Perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian

In physical coordinates, the perturbed Lagrangian is given by

\bar{L}_{1}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q{\bm{A}}_{1}({\bm{R}},t)\bcdot\dot{{\bm{R}}}-q\phi_{1}({\bm{R}},t),

(3.1)

where
{\bm{A}}_{1}
and
\phi_{1}
are the perturbed vector and scalar potentials resulting in the perturbed electric and magnetic field which are assumed to be small compared to
{\bm{B}}_{0}
, i.e.
\varepsilon_{\delta}\ll 1
.
Note that we have added a superscripted
†
which we have introduced to distinguish this Lagrangian from the final perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian in which we have subtracted the total derivative of some function.

Using
Eq.

2.19
we find that

{\bm{R}}=\bar{{\bm{R}}}+{\bm{\rho}},

(3.2)

which expresses the particle position
{\bm{R}}
in terms of the guiding-centre position
\bar{{\bm{R}}}
and the radial vector
{\bm{\rho}}
.
We introduce the following compact notation to indicate the evaluation of a scalar function, the gradient of a scalar function, or a vector field at the particle position

\mathring{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=Q(\bar{{\bm{R}}}+{\bm{\rho}}),\quad\mathring{\nabla}Q\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\nabla Q)(\bar{{\bm{R}}}+{\bm{\rho}}),\quad\mathring{S}_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{S}}(\bar{{\bm{R}}}+{\bm{\rho}})\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}(\bar{{\bm{R}}}).

(3.3)

When considering
Fig.

1
one might expect that the coordinate vector
{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}
should be evaluated at the particle position
\bar{{\bm{R}}}+{\bm{{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}}}
.
However, from the derivation of the model it turns out that the evaluation is always done at the guiding-centre position
\bar{{\bm{R}}}
, which is equivalent to the evaluation at the particle position up to an
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contribution.

When making use of
Eq.

3.3
it follows that
Eq.

3.1
can be rewritten as

\bar{L}_{1}^{\dagger}=q\mathring{{\bm{A}}}_{1}\bcdot(\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\dot{{\bm{\rho}}})-q\mathring{\phi}_{1}.

(3.4)

Note that both
{\bm{\rho}}
and
\mathring{{\bm{A}}}_{1}
(via
{\bm{\rho}}
) depend on the gyro-phase
\bar{\Theta}
, and therefore we are in need of a third coordinate transformation which is aimed at removing the gyro-phase dependence of the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian and results in the gyrocentre phase-space coordinates
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}=(\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}},\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel},\bar{\bar{M}},\bar{\bar{\Theta}})
.

3.2
Gyrocentre coordinate transformation

Before we can perform the gyrocentre coordinate transformation, however, we must briefly discuss Lie transformations
(
Littlejohn 1982
;
Cary & Littlejohn 1983
;
Dragt & Finn 1976
)
, which will be used for this purpose.
We consider second-order Lie transformations, which are phase-space coordinate transformations of the form

\bar{{\bm{Z}}}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}-\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2},

(3.5)

where
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}
are the first- and second-order generating vectors.

The resulting gyrocentre Lagrangian is defined such that

\bar{\bar{L}}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}},\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}})=\bar{L}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}},\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}})+\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{S}}}{\mathrm{d}t}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}^{3}),

(3.6)

where we have added the total derivative of a generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}=\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}
to the gyrocentre Lagrangian

\bar{\bar{L}}\mapsto\bar{\bar{L}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{S}}}{\mathrm{d}t},

(3.7)

resulting in the following additions to the Hamiltonian and symplectic part

\bar{\bar{H}}\mapsto\bar{\bar{H}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}}{\partial t},\quad\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}\mapsto\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}+\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}.

(3.8)

This results in the following gyrocentre Hamiltonian
\bar{\bar{H}}=\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\bar{H}_{0},

(3.9a)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{H}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t},

(3.9b)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{H}_{2}-\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}-\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\bar{H}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\right]\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}}{\partial t},

(3.9c)

whereas the symplectic part
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
is given by

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0},

(3.10a)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}+\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}},

(3.10b)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{2}+\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1})\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}.

(3.10c)

We use the Lagrange matrix
\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}
as given by
Eqs.

2.30
and
2.34
and have equivalently defined the perturbed Lagrange matrices as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}},

(3.11a)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\right]^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right).

(3.11b)

These transformation rules are based on Taylor series expansions and have been derived in
Appendix

B
.
Note that the contribution due to the first-order generating function vanishes in
Eq.

3.11b
because the skew-symmetric part of the Hessian matrix of
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
vanishes.

3.3
The general form of the gyrocentre coordinate transformation

The generating vectors
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}
, which are used to define the gyrocentre coordinate transformation, are chosen to satisfy some desired form of the symplectic part
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1},\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
of the Lagrangian, by inverting
Eqs.

3.10b
and
3.10c
, respectively.
This yields a transformation of the Hamiltonian part of the Lagrangian, as given by
Eq.

3.9
, where the generating vectors will re-introduce gyro-phase dependence in the gyrocentre Hamiltonian.
The role of the generating functions
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}
is to absorb the gyro-phase dependent part of the resulting gyrocentre Hamiltonian.

3.3.1
The first-order transformation

Without specifying the desired form of
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}
, we find that this approach results in the following first-order generating vector field

\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}=\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right),

(3.12)

which, upon substitution in
Eq.

3.9b
, results in the following first-order Hamiltonian

\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}=\bar{H}_{1}+\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\right)\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\{\bar{\bar{S}}_{1},\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}\}_{0}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t}=q\psi_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t}-\{\bar{\bar{S}}_{1},\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}\}_{0},

(3.13)

where we have used
Eqs.

2.29
and
2.32
, denote by
\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
the unperturbed guiding-centre EOMs (
2.42
) evaluated at the gyrocentre coordinate
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
, and we have defined the effective potential as

q\psi_{1}=\bar{H}_{1}+\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\right)\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}.

(3.14)

We now let the generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
absorb the gyro-phase dependent part of
\psi_{1}
, such that
Eq.

3.13
results in

\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t}+\{\bar{\bar{S}}_{1},\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}\}_{0}=q\widetilde{\psi}_{1}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}=q\langle\psi_{1}\rangle,

(3.15)

where we define the gyro-average and the resulting gyro-phase dependent part of some function
Q
as

\langle Q\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}Q\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{\Theta}},\quad\widetilde{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=Q-\langle Q\rangle.

(3.16)

It follows that the first-order part of the single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian is given by

\bar{\bar{L}}_{1}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}+\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}\rangle\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\langle\bar{H}_{1}\rangle.

(3.17)

It is insightful to consider the two limiting cases of
Eq.

3.17
: if
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}={\bm{0}}_{6}
then the gyrocentre coordinate transformation transforms the entire symplectic part of the first-order guiding-centre Lagrangian to the Hamiltonian part of the Lagrangian (this is referred to as the Hamiltonian formulation)

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}={\bm{0}}_{6}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}=\langle\bar{H}_{1}\rangle-\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\rangle\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}

(3.18a)

and conversely if
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}=\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\rangle
then the symplectic and Hamiltonian parts of the first-order guiding-centre Lagrangian simply end up being gyro-averaged

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}=\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\rangle\quad\implies\quad\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}=\langle\bar{H}_{1}\rangle.

(3.18b)

3.3.2
The second-order transformation

We follow the same approach for deriving the second-order Hamiltonian.
That is, we solve
Eq.

3.10c
for
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}
resulting in

\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}=\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\left[\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1})\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right],

(3.19)

without specifying
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
and by making use of
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{2}={\bm{0}}_{6}
.
This allows us to express the second-order Hamiltonian (
3.9c
) in the following way

\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}+{\bm{T}}_{1}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}}{\partial t}-\{\bar{\bar{S}}_{2},\bar{H}_{0}\}_{0},

(3.20)

where we have made use of
\bar{H}_{2}=0
, and we have defined

{\bm{T}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1})\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\bar{H}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)

(3.21)

by making use of
Eq.

2.29
.

As with the first-order generating function, the second-order generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}
will be defined such that it absorbs the gyro-phase dependent part of
\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}
resulting in

\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{2}}{\partial t}+\{\bar{\bar{S}}_{2},\bar{H}_{0}\}_{0}=\widetilde{\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}+\widetilde{{\bm{T}}_{1}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}

(3.22)

and therefore

\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}=\langle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}\rangle\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}+\langle{\bm{T}}_{1}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\rangle.

(3.23)

To summarize, we have thus far considered a general gyrocentre coordinate transformation, where we are still free to choose the symplectic parts
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
.
The resulting first- and second-order Hamiltonians are given by

\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}=\langle\bar{H}_{1}\rangle+\langle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\rangle\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}

(3.24)

as well as
Eq.

3.23
, respectively as follows from
Eqs.

3.14
and
3.15
.
For consistency, we require that
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
are
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta})
and
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}^{2})
, respectively.
Moreover, as the purpose of the gyrocentre coordinate transformation is to decouple the gyro-phase from the perturbed Lagrangian, we must also require
\widetilde{\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}}=\widetilde{\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}}={\bm{0}}_{6}
.
In what follows, we discuss a third requirement on
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
, which ensures that the resulting single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian is gauge invariant.

3.4
Gauge invariance

Gauge invariance refers to invariance under the gauge transformation

\phi_{1}\mapsto\phi_{1}-\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial t},\quad{\bm{A}}_{1}\mapsto{\bm{A}}_{1}+\nabla\eta

(3.25)

for some scalar function
\eta
.
The electromagnetic fields, as given by

{\bm{E}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-\nabla\phi_{1}-\frac{\partial{\bm{A}}_{1}}{\partial t},\quad{\bm{B}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nabla\times{\bm{A}}_{1},

(3.26)

are invariant under the gauge transformation (
3.25
), from which it follows that any (part of a) model which is expressed in terms of the electromagnetic fields is automatically gauge invariant.
If a model is gauge invariant, it means that it does not matter which gauge condition is used to fix the function
\eta
, which is what we would expect from a physical point of view.

Following the discussion in
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
, we introduce the following parametrized perturbed Lagrangian

{\bar{L}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\bcdot\left(\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\varsigma\dot{{\bm{\rho}}}\right)-q\mathring{\phi}^{\varsigma}_{1},

(3.27)

where we have defined

\mathring{Q}^{\varsigma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=Q(\bar{{\bm{R}}}+\varsigma{\bm{\rho}}).

(3.28)

The
\varsigma
parameter therefore interpolates from the guiding-centre position (
\varsigma=0
) to the particle position (
\varsigma=1
), see also
Fig.

1
.
It follows that the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian, as given by
Eq.

3.1
, coincides with
\varsigma=1
and can therefore be written as

\bar{L}_{1}^{\dagger}=\bar{L}_{1}^{\varsigma=1}=\bar{L}_{1}^{\varsigma=0}+(\bar{L}_{1}^{\varsigma=1}-\bar{L}_{1}^{\varsigma=0})=\underbrace{\bar{L}_{1}^{\varsigma=0}}_{\bar{L}_{1}^{\dagger,\mathrm{ZLR}}}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bar{L}}^{\varsigma}_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\varsigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma}_{\bar{L}_{1}^{\dagger,\mathrm{FLR}}},

(3.29)

which can therefore be written as the sum of a zero Larmor radius (ZLR) contribution and a finite Larmor radius (FLR) contribution.

Computation of the
\varsigma
derivative of the parametrized Lagrangian yields

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bar{L}}^{\varsigma}_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\varsigma}=q(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}{\bm{A}}_{1}{\bm{\rho}})\bcdot(\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\varsigma\dot{{\bm{\rho}}})+q\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\bcdot\dot{{\bm{\rho}}}-q\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\phi_{1}\bcdot{\bm{\rho}}.

(3.30)

Furthermore, we note that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1})=\dot{{\bm{\rho}}}\bcdot\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}+{\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\bm{A}}(\bar{{\bm{R}}}+\varsigma{\bm{\rho}},t)=\dot{{\bm{\rho}}}\bcdot\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}+{\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\left[\frac{\partial\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}}{\partial t}+\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}{\bm{A}}_{1}(\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\varsigma\dot{{\bm{\rho}}})\right],

(3.31)

from which it follows that

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bar{L}}^{\varsigma}_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\varsigma}-q\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1})=q{\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\left[(\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\varsigma\dot{{\bm{\rho}}})\times\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}+\mathring{{\bm{E}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\right],

(3.32)

and therefore the FLR part of the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian can, up to a total derivative, be expressed in terms of the gauge invariant electromagnetic fields.

In what follows, we omit the contribution by the total derivative, as this does not alter the resulting EOMs after imposing the principle of least action.
We denote the resulting perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian by
\bar{L}_{1}
for which

\bar{L}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{L}_{1}^{\dagger}-q\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{0}^{1}{\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\mathring{{\bm{A}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma.

(3.33)

And therefore

\bar{L}_{1}=(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}+\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}})\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-(\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}+\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}),

(3.34a)

where the symplectic part
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}+\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}
is given by

\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q{\bm{A}}_{1},\quad\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}^{\mathrm{FLR}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q\int_{0}^{1}\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\times{\bm{\rho}},\quad\bar{\gamma}_{1,\Theta}^{\mathrm{FLR}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-q\rho^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\varsigma\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\bcdot{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0},

(3.34b)

the Hamiltonian part
\bar{H}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}+\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}
is given by

\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q\phi_{1},\quad\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-q{\bm{\rho}}\bcdot\int_{0}^{1}\mathring{{\bm{E}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(3.34c)

and we have neglected the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contribution.

We distinguish the ZLR contributions from the FLR contributions.
Note that each of the FLR contributions is gauge invariant, as they are expressed in terms of the electromagnetic fields.

When considering the Lie coordinate transformation given by
Eqs.

3.10
and
3.9
, we find that the following yields a sufficient condition for gauge invariance of the resulting gyrokinetic model.
A proof can be found in
Appendix

C
.

Theorem 1
(Sufficient condition for gauge invariance)

The single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian (to second order) is gauge invariant provided that
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
are gauge invariant.

3.5
A family of gauge invariant gyrocentre coordinate transformations

Thus far, we have considered a general gyrocentre coordinate transformation, which is defined by the symplectic part of the gyrocentre Lagrangian:
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
.
In what follows, we let
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}={\bm{0}}_{6}
.
Therefore, we find that consistency (w.r.t. the Lie transformation), gyro-phase independence and gauge invariance of the resulting gyrocentre Lagrangian requires

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}),\quad\widetilde{\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}}={\bm{0}}_{6},\quad\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:gauge_transform}}}{\mapsto}\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1},

(3.35)

respectively.

3.5.1
Overview

Traditionally
(
Brizard 1990
;
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
, the following choice was made

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}=q\langle\mathring{{\bm{A}}}_{1}\rangle,

(3.36)

with all other components equal to zero.
Note that this corresponds to
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}=\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}^{\dagger}\rangle
, where
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\dagger}
denotes the symplectic part of the guiding-centre Lagrangian before the total derivative has been omitted, see also
Eq.

3.33
.
This choice satisfies the first and second of our three requirements, but it does not lead to a gauge invariant model:

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:gauge_transform}}}{\mapsto}\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}+q(\langle\mathring{\nabla}\eta\rangle-\nabla\eta).

(3.37)

This approach leads to a gyrokinetic model in which compressional Alfvén waves can be included by considering a high-frequency approximation for the first-order generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
, as proposed in
(
Qin et al. 1999
)
.

More recently, the following gyrocentre coordinate transformation was proposed in
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}=q{\bm{A}}_{1},

(3.38)

which satisfies all three of our requirements since

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}=-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}},

(3.39)

which is gauge invariant as the FLR parts of the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian are gauge invariant.
Rather than keeping only the ZLR part of the symplectic part of the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian, we can also include the FLR effects resulting in

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}+\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}\rangle=\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\rangle.

(3.40)

Gauge invariance follows from the gauge invariance of
\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}
, and we have gyro-averaged the FLR contribution to ensure that our second requirement is satisfied.
We note that
Eq.

3.40
results in a transformation for which the first-order generating vector is, in some sense, smallest.
This is of interest because the gyrokinetic model results from a
truncated
coordinate transformation, where the truncation error is smaller if the coordinate transformation is smaller.
In particular, using
Eq.

3.12
we find that

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}=\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\rangle\quad\implies\quad\langle\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\rangle=\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\left\langle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right\rangle={\bm{0}}_{6},

(3.41)

and therefore the coordinate transformation given by
Eq.

3.40
contains, to first order, only a fluctuating gyro-phase dependent part.

Henceforth, we consider the following parametrized form of the symplectic part of the gyrocentre Lagrangian

\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{pmatrix}q{\bm{A}}_{1}+\xi_{R}q\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle\\
0\\
0\\
-\xi_{\Theta}\frac{q\rho^{2}}{2}\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle\end{pmatrix},

(3.42)

where
\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta}
are real-valued parameters that define the coordinate transformation.
The choice
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(0,0)
yields the model proposed in
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
,
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,1)
results in
Eq.

3.40
, and we note that this general form is gauge invariant regardless of the value of
\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta}
.
Here we have defined
the radially averaged gyro-average as

\langle\!|\mathring{Q}^{\varsigma}|\!\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\mathring{Q}^{\varsigma}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}Q(\bar{\bar{{\bm{r}}}}+\varsigma{\bm{\rho}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma

(3.43)

and the disc average as (cf.
Eq.

3.28
)

\langle\!\langle\mathring{Q}^{\varsigma}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=2\langle\!|\varsigma\mathring{Q}^{\varsigma}|\!\rangle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varsigma Q(\bar{\bar{{\bm{r}}}}+\varsigma{\bm{\rho}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma.

(3.44)

The latter operator is referred to as the disc average because it exactly yields the average value of the ‘gyro-disc’ shown in
Fig.

1
.

The parametrized coordinate transformation results in the following first-order gyrocentre Lagrangian

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{L}}_{1}={}

\displaystyle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}+\langle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}^{\mathrm{FLR}}\rangle\bcdot\left[\xi_{R}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}+(1-\xi_{R})\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}\right]+\langle\bar{\gamma}_{1,\Theta}^{\mathrm{FLR}}\rangle\left[\xi_{\Theta}\dot{\bar{\bar{\Theta}}}+(1-\xi_{\Theta})\dot{\bar{\Theta}}\right]

\displaystyle-q\phi_{1}+q\rho\int_{0}^{1}\langle\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(3.45)

where we have substituted
Eqs.

3.42
and
3.34c
into
Eq.

3.24
.
This shows that the parameters
\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta}
put the symplectic FLR part of the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian either in the symplectic (
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,1)
) or in the Hamiltonian (
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(0,0)
) part of the first-order gyrocentre Lagrangian.

The EOM for
\bar{\bar{M}}
can already be computed by making use of the Euler–Lagrange equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{L}}}{\partial\dot{\Theta}}=\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{L}}}{\partial\Theta}\quad\implies\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{\gamma}}_{0,\Theta}}{\mathrm{d}t}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{\gamma}}_{1,\Theta}}{\mathrm{d}t}=0\quad\implies\quad\dot{\bar{\bar{M}}}=\xi_{\Theta}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\bar{\bar{M}}\frac{\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle}{B_{0}}\right),

(3.46)

where we have assumed
\bar{\bar{\gamma}}_{2,\Theta}=0
.
Hence, requiring
\bar{\bar{M}}
to remain invariant in gyrocentre coordinates implies that
\xi_{\Theta}=0
, which we will use from here on out.

3.5.2
The first-order transformation

The first-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian is found by substituting
Eqs.

2.42
and
3.34b
into
Eq.

3.45

\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q\phi_{1}-q\rho\langle\!|\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}|\!\rangle-(1-\xi_{R})q\rho\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}\langle\!|\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}|\!\rangle+\bar{\bar{M}}\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle,

(3.47)

where we have neglected the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contribution from
\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}
.

We need an explicit expression for the first-order generating vector
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
for the computation of the second-order Hamiltonian as follows from
Eq.

3.23
.
Recall that the first-order generating vector
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
is given by
Eq.

3.12
, which itself requires an expression for the first-order generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
.
From
Eq.

3.15
it follows that

\underbrace{\frac{1}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t}}_{\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\omega})}+\underbrace{\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{\Theta}}}}_{\mathcal{O}(1)}+\underbrace{\frac{\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot\nabla\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}_{\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\omega})}=\frac{q}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}\widetilde{\psi}_{1},

(3.48)

where we have substituted the zeroth-order Hamiltonian, as given by
Eq.

2.21
and have neglected the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contributions from the guiding-centre Poisson bracket (
2.5
).
Furthermore, we have indicated the magnitude of each of the terms, which is a result from nondimensionalization using

[t]=\frac{1}{\omega},\quad[U_{\shortparallel}]=\frac{\omega}{k_{\shortparallel}},

(3.49)

where we recall that the nondimensional frequency
\varepsilon_{\omega}
is defined in
Eq.

2.17
.
Using
Eqs.

3.14
and
3.34
we find that

q\widetilde{\psi}_{1}=\widetilde{\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}}-\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\bcdot\widetilde{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}}=-\rho\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma+\omega_{\mathrm{c}}q\rho^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\varsigma\widetilde{\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(3.50)

where we have introduced the Lorentz force

{\bm{F}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q\left({\bm{E}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{B}}_{1}\right),\quad\mathring{{\bm{F}}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q\left(\mathring{{\bm{E}}_{1}}+\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\mathring{{\bm{B}}}_{1}\right).

(3.51)

When considering approximations of
Eq.

3.48
it is important to keep gauge invariance of the resulting model in mind.
In particular, when considering the proof of
Theorem

1
as given in
Appendix

C
, we find that gauge invariance of the first-order generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
is needed, and this is proven by observing that
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
is the solution of a linear PDE (
3.15
) with a gauge invariant right-hand side given by
Eq.

3.50
.
When obtaining an approximation to
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
it is therefore essential that we preserve its gauge invariance, which can rather easily be achieved by simply keeping the gauge invariant parts of the right-hand side
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
together.

We now make several long wavelength approximations to
Eq.

3.50
, starting with

\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}=F_{1,\rho}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp})\quad\implies\quad\widetilde{\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}}=\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}-\langle\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}\rangle=F_{1,\rho}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp}),

(3.52)

as follows from a Taylor series expansion of
{\bm{F}}_{1}
centred around the gyrocentre position
\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}
(see the discussion in
Appendix

D
), where we recall that the nondimensional perpendicular wave number
\varepsilon_{\perp}
is as defined in
Eq.

2.18
.
Similarly, we find that

\widetilde{\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}}=\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}-\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp})

(3.53)

and therefore neglecting
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp})
contributions to the right-hand side of
Eq.

3.50
and neglecting the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\omega})
part of the left-hand side of
Eq.

3.48
, we find that the first-order generating function can be approximated by

\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}=-\frac{\rho}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}F_{1,\rho}\quad\iff\quad\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}=\frac{\rho}{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}F_{1,\tau}.

(3.54)

This allows us to approximate the first-order generating vector explicitly as

\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}=\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}_{0}\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{B_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\int_{0}^{1}\left(\xi_{R}\langle\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}\rangle-\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma+\frac{\rho B_{1,\rho}}{B_{0}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\\
-\frac{q}{m}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot\int_{0}^{1}\left(\xi_{R}\langle\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}\rangle-\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\\
-\frac{q^{2}\rho^{2}}{m}\int_{0}^{1}\varsigma\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma-\frac{\rho}{B_{0}}F_{1,\rho}\\
-\frac{\rho}{2B_{0}\bar{\bar{M}}}F_{1,\tau}\end{pmatrix},

(3.55)

where we have substituted
Eqs.

3.54
,
3.42
and
3.34b
into
Eq.

3.12
and have furthermore neglected all spatial derivatives of the perturbed electromagnetic fields for the evaluation of the Poisson bracket
\{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}},\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}\}_{0}
.

3.5.3
The second-order transformation

When assuming
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}={\bm{0}}_{6}
, as we do throughout this discussion, we find that
Eq.

3.23
results in

\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}=\langle{\bm{T}}_{1}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\rangle,

(3.56)

where
{\bm{T}}_{1}
is as defined in
Eq.

3.21
.
The approximation of
{\bm{T}}_{1}
, followed by substitution of the approximated first-order generating vector
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
and subsequent gyro-averaging results in the following second-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian

\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\bar{\bar{M}}}{2B_{0}}\lvert{\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}-\frac{m}{2q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}\lvert{\bm{F}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}+(1-\xi_{R})^{2}\frac{q^{2}\rho^{2}}{2m}\langle\!|\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}|\!\rangle^{2},

(3.57)

which, except for the FLR contribution, agrees with the result from
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
(hence with
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(0,0)
) upon substitution of the expression for the Lorentz force (
3.51
).
The derivation of
Eq.

3.57
is rather tedious and can be found in
Appendix

E
.

3.6
Principle of least action

For the derivation of the EOMs we follow the same approach as followed in
Section

2.5
, and therefore we must compute the perturbed gyrocentre Lagrange matrix
\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1}
, which follows from the skew-symmetric part of the Jacobian matrix of
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}
as defined in
Eq.

3.42
.
We note that the unperturbed gyrocentre Lagrange matrix coincides with the unperturbed guiding-centre Lagrange matrix
\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}
, given by
Eq.

2.34
, except that it is now evaluated at gyrocentre coordinates.
From
Eq.

3.42
it follows that

\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\begin{pmatrix}q\nabla{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}&{\bm{0}}_{3}&\xi_{R}\frac{q}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}\left[\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\!\langle(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}{\bm{B}}_{1}{\bm{\rho}})\times{\bm{\rho}}\rangle\!\rangle\right]&{\bm{0}}_{3}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&0\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&0\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(3.58)

where we have defined the FLR corrected vector potential as

{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{A}}_{1}+\xi_{R}\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle.

(3.59)

We refer to
{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}
as the FLR corrected vector potential because the corresponding FLR corrected magnetic field
{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nabla\times{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}
can be approximated as

{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}_{1}^{\xi_{R}=1}=\nabla\times{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}^{\xi_{R}=1}=\nabla\times{\bm{A}}_{1}+\nabla\times\langle\!|(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{A}}_{1})\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle=\langle\mathring{{\bm{B}}}_{1}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})

(3.60)

by making use of
Eq.

D.12
and assuming
\xi_{R}=1
.

For the computation of the gyrocentre Poisson bracket, we must invert the gyrocentre Lagrange matrix.
The resulting Lagrange matrix is given by (cf.
Eq.

2.34
)

\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}=\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}^{\star}&-m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}&\frac{m}{q}{\bm{w}}&{\bm{0}}_{3}\\
m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&0\\
-\frac{m}{q}{\bm{w}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&\frac{m}{q}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&-\frac{m}{q}&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(3.61)

where we have defined the effective gyrocentre potential as (see also
Eq.

2.22
)

{\bm{A}}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{A}}_{0}^{\star}+{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}={\bm{A}}_{0}+{\bm{A}}_{1}+\frac{m\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}{q}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}-\frac{m\bar{M}}{q^{2}}{\bm{w}}_{0}+\xi_{R}\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle,\quad{\bm{B}}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nabla\times{\bm{A}}^{\star}

(3.62)

as well as

{\bm{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{w}}_{0}-\xi_{R}\frac{q^{2}}{2m\bar{\bar{M}}}\left[\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\!\langle(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}{\bm{B}}_{1}{\bm{\rho}})\times{\bm{\rho}}\rangle\!\rangle\right].

(3.63)

The matrix
\mathsfbi{B}^{\star}
is defined analogously to
Eq.

2.36
.

Using the result of
Appendix

A
we find that the gyrocentre Poisson matrix is given by

\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathsfbi{B}_{0}}{qB_{0}B_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}&\frac{{\bm{b}}^{\star}}{m}&{\bm{0}}_{3}&-\frac{{\bm{w}}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}}{qB_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}\\
-\frac{({\bm{b}}^{\star})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{m}&0&0&-\frac{{\bm{b}}^{\star}\bcdot{\bm{w}}}{m}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&-\frac{q}{m}\\
\frac{({\bm{w}}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{qB_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}&\frac{{\bm{b}}^{\star}\bcdot{\bm{w}}}{m}&\frac{q}{m}&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(3.64)

where we have defined (cf.
Eq.

2.38
)

{\bm{b}}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{{\bm{B}}^{\star}}{B_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}={\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}+\frac{1}{B_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}\left[\frac{m\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}{q}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{m\bar{\bar{M}}}{q^{2}}(\nabla\times{\bm{w}}_{0})_{\perp}+{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}_{1,\perp}\right],

(3.65)

and note that
B_{\shortparallel}^{\star}
can be written explicitly as

B_{\shortparallel}^{\star}=B_{0}+B_{1,\shortparallel}+\xi_{R}(\nabla\times\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle)_{\shortparallel}+\frac{m\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}{q}(\nabla\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})_{\shortparallel}-\frac{m\bar{\bar{M}}}{q^{2}}(\nabla\times{\bm{w}}_{0})_{\shortparallel}.

(3.66)

Analogous to
Eq.

2.32
, we define the gyrocentre Poisson bracket as

\{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\left(\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right),

(3.67)

such that the EOMs, similar to
Eq.

2.33
, are given by

\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{J}}}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}}{\partial t}+\{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}},\bar{\bar{H}}\},

(3.68)

where the zeroth-order term
\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}
of the Hamiltonian is defined in
Eq.

2.21
, the first-order term
\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}
is defined in
Eq.

3.47
, and the second-order term is given by
Eq.

3.57
.
Substitution of
Eq.

3.64
in
Eq.

3.68
results in

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{H}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}{\bm{b}}^{\star}+\frac{1}{qB_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\left(\nabla\bar{\bar{H}}+q\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right),

(3.69a)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\bar{U}}}_{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{m}{\bm{b}}^{\star}\bcdot\left(\nabla\bar{\bar{H}}+q\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right),

(3.69b)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\bar{M}}}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.69c)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\bar{\Theta}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{m}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{H}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{M}}}+{\bm{w}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\,.

(3.69d)

Here we note that, even though the EOM for the gyro-phase
\bar{\bar{\Theta}}
is non-trivial, it does not have to be solved as none of the other terms on the right-hand side of the EOMs depend on the gyro-phase.

4
Gyrokinetic Vlasov–Maxwell model

Thus far, we have derived a gyrokinetic model for single-particle motion for a given electromagnetic field, which includes a time-dependent perturbation.
This forms the basis for a gyrokinetic approximation of the coupled and self-consistent Vlasov–Maxwell system of equations, wherein the time-dependent perturbation of the electromagnetic field results from the motion of the charged particles themselves.

In this section we first introduce the particle distribution function for each of the species, which we then use to formulate the self-consistent action principle.
Provided with this action principle we then derive the resulting EOMs for the particles as well as the corresponding field equations for the electromagnetic field.
The field equations are considered in a strong formulation wherein we recognize the macroscopic Maxwell’s equations.
We discuss equilibrium solutions as well as well-posedness of the field equations.
The section is concluded with a discussion on energy conservation.

As we will exclusively discuss the gyrokinetic model, which is expressed in gyrocentre coordinates, we will drop the
\bar{\bar{\cdot}}
notation and simply write
{\bm{Z}}
rather than
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
.

4.1
Particle distribution function

Several particle species are considered, which we denote by the subscript
s
, where usually
s\in\{\mathrm{i},\mathrm{e}\}
for the ion species
\mathrm{i}
and the electron species
\mathrm{e}
.
Each species has its own particle mass
m_{s}
and charge
q_{s}
.
A particle distribution function is considered, for each particle species
s
, which is denoted by
f_{s}({\bm{r}},u_{\shortparallel},\mu,t)
and coincides with the number of particles per unit phase-space volume.
Note that the particle distribution function does not depend on the gyro-phase, as the gyrocentre EOMs given by
Eq.

3.69
do not depend on this decoupled phase-space coordinate.
We use the small-case letter
{\bm{z}}=({\bm{r}},u_{\shortparallel},\mu,\theta)
to refer to the Eulerian equivalent of the Lagrangian phase-space coordinate
{\bm{Z}}
.

We denote the dependence of a particle’s Lagrangian characteristic on the initial phase-space coordinate
{\bm{z}}^{0}=({\bm{R}}(t^{0}),U_{\shortparallel}(t^{0}),M(t^{0}),\Theta(t^{0}))
in the following way (in the absence of collisions)

{\bm{Z}}(t)={\bm{Z}}(t;{\bm{z}}^{0},t^{0}).

(4.1)

The particle distribution function then satisfies (by definition)

f_{s}({\bm{Z}}(t;{\bm{z}}^{0},t^{0}),t)=f^{0}_{s}({\bm{z}}^{0}),

(4.2)

where we denote by
f^{0}_{s}
the particle distribution function at
t^{0}
.
Hence, the following Vlasov equation is satisfied

\frac{\mathrm{d}f_{s}}{\mathrm{d}t}=0\quad\iff\quad\frac{\partial f_{s}}{\partial t}+\dot{{\bm{R}}}\bcdot\nabla f_{s}+\dot{U}_{\shortparallel}\frac{\partial f_{s}}{\partial u_{\shortparallel}}=0,

(4.3)

where we now consider the EOMs
\dot{{\bm{Z}}}
given by
Eq.

3.69
to be evaluated at the Eulerian phase-space coordinate
{\bm{z}}
.

Recall that the physical coordinates, as defined in
Section

2.1
, were denoted by
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
.
Our field-theoretic Lagrangian, which will be discussed in
Section

4.2
, is formulated using integrals over physical space, which will have to be transformed to integrals over the gyrocentre coordinates.
For instance, we consider the integral of a function
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{v}},t)=\mathcal{F}({\bm{r}},u_{\shortparallel},\mu,\theta,t)
(note that we now write
{\bm{x}},{\bm{v}}
for the physical position and velocity, to distinguish them from the gyrocentre position and velocity, which are now denoted by
{\bm{r}},{\bm{u}}
since we have omitted the
\bar{\bar{\cdot}}
notation)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\Omega}\tilde{\mathcal{F}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}v=\int\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}z,

(4.4)

where the integration limits and differentials are defined as

\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}z\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int\int_{\Omega}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}u,\quad\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}u\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u_{\shortparallel}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta,

(4.5)

and
\mathfrak{J}_{s}
