(5.22)

Since the right-hand side does not vanish in general we find that a result analogous to
Eq.

4.48
is not obtained.

Upon inspecting
Eq.

5.17
, we find that the bound current is given by

{\bm{\mathcal{J}}}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{BH}}={\bm{\mathcal{J}}}^{\mathrm{m},\mathrm{BH}}=\sum_{s}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\nabla\bcdot({\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,s}^{\shortparallel}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}),

(5.23)

which is not divergence free.
Computation of the divergence of Ampère’s law (
5.18a
), with the contribution from the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, results in the constraint

0=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-3.68056pt\hbox{$\mathcal{R}$}\hskip-3.68056pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}+\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{BH}})+\nabla\bcdot(\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.56944pt\hbox{${\bm{\mathcal{J}}}$}\hskip-2.56944pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f},\mathrm{BH}}+{\bm{\mathcal{J}}}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{BH}}+\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda),

(5.24)

upon addition of the quasi-neutrality equation (
5.18b
) where we let
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{BH}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-\sum_{s}\nabla\bcdot{\bm{\mathcal{P}}}_{1,s}^{\shortparallel}
.
In this case, the gyro-average adjoint of the free charge continuity equation does not vanish, and we are therefore left with a total continuity equation which is enforced by means of a Lagrange multiplier.
Due to
Eq.

5.22
we find that the constraint
Eq.

5.24
also depends on the characteristics
{\bm{Z}}
, which is very undesirable as it implies that the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
also affects the EOMs.

Hence, for the symplectic Brizard–Hahm model
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
we find that
\lambda\neq 0
for three independent reasons: the polarization current is missing, the magnetization current is not the curl of a magnetization, and the gyro-average adjoint of the free charge continuity equation does not hold.
Note that a polarization current can be included by considering higher-order approximations of the first-order generating function, see e.g.
(
Qin et al. 1999
)
.

5.3
Gyrokinetic Darwin approximation

The proposed gauge-invariant gyrokinetic model keeps more physics than the popular reduced parallel-only model discussed in
Section

5.1
and has more structure than the fully-symplectic Brizard–Hahm model
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
.
In particular, we find that Ampère’s law (
4.38
) now contains a polarization current, which is not present in either of the two other models.
Part of this polarization current, however, gives rise to a fast compressional Alfvén wave as will be demonstrated in
Section

5.4
, and in most situations such waves are undesired due to their high frequency.

One way to deal with such high frequency components is to damp them numerically using implicit time integration methods.
Another option is to remove such waves from the underlying model, i.e. from the Lagrangian.
For instance, to remove light-speed waves from Maxwell’s equations, rather than considering the limit of quasi-neutrality, one can consider the Darwin approximation, wherein only the contribution due to the scalar potential is kept in the
\epsilon_{0}\partial{\bm{E}}_{1}/\partial t
term in Ampère’s law.
Thus, the solenoidal, i.e. divergence free, part (when assuming that the Coulomb gauge is used) of the displacement current is neglected, resulting in a model which yields a second- and third-order accurate electric and magnetic field, respectively, in the small parameter
\varepsilon_{c}=v/c

(
Degond & Raviart 1992
)
.

5.3.1
The Hamiltonian

We follow an approach similar to the Darwin approximation to remove the fast compressional Alfvén waves.
Recall that the second-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian is given by (cf.
Eq.

3.57
, with
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,0)
)

H_{2,s}=-\frac{m_{s}}{2B_{0}^{2}}\lvert{\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}+\frac{m_{s}U_{\shortparallel}}{B_{0}^{2}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot({\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})+\frac{MB_{0}-m_{s}U_{\shortparallel}^{2}}{2B_{0}^{2}}{\lvert{\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}},

(5.25)

where we have substituted the definition of the Lorentz force (
3.51
), and this result thereby agrees exactly with
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
, Eq. (14))
.
We note that the compressional Alfvén wave comes from the
\lvert\partial{\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}/\partial t\rvert^{2}
term (which itself comes from the
\lvert{\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}
term), and it should therefore be sufficient to remove exactly this term from
H_{2,s}
.

However, when well-posedness of the resulting Darwin model is considered we find that the
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot({\bm{E}}_{1}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})
term from
H_{2,s}
must also be omitted.
The contribution to the field Lagrangian due to the second-order part of the Hamiltonian involves integration over the background particle distribution function and therefore omitting this term is justified whenever

\sum_{s}m_{s}\int f^{0}_{s}u_{\shortparallel}\mathfrak{J}_{00,s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}u\approx 0.

(5.26)

Note that this constraint is satisfied whenever we consider a nearly symmetric background distribution as given by
Eq.

4.51
with
\varepsilon_{U,s}\ll 1
.
As we require
\varepsilon_{U,s}\ll 1
already for justifying
\varepsilon_{\delta}\ll 1
, we find that the term can be omitted from
H_{2,s}
without introducing any additional assumptions.

When removing both the compressional Alfvén wave and the
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot({\bm{E}}_{1}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})
term from
H_{2,s}
we find

H_{2,s}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-\frac{m_{s}}{2B_{0}^{2}}\left(\lvert\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}\rvert^{2}+2\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}\bcdot\frac{\partial{\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}}{\partial t}\right)+\frac{MB_{0}-m_{s}U_{\shortparallel}^{2}}{2B_{0}^{2}}{\lvert{\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}},

(5.27)

where we note that this an approximation breaks gauge invariance.

5.3.2
Principle of least action

As the particle part of the Hamiltonian is still given by
Eq.

4.17b
, and the symplectic part of the Lagrangian is unchanged when compared to the gauge invariant model, we find that the EOMs are still given by
Eq.

4.28
.

The explicit expression of Low’s action, while using
Eq.

5.27
and omitting the contribution due to the gauge condition (cf.
Eq.

4.30
), is given by

\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{Dar}}({\bm{Z}},\phi_{1},{\bm{A}}_{1},\lambda)=\sum_{s}\int f^{0}_{s}({\bm{z}}^{0})\biggl[q_{s}\left({\bm{A}}_{0,s}^{\star}+{\bm{A}}_{1}+\langle\!|\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}|\!\rangle\right)\bcdot\dot{{\bm{R}}}+\frac{m_{s}M}{q_{s}}\dot{\Theta}\\
-M(B_{0}+\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle)-\frac{m_{s}}{2}U_{\shortparallel}^{2}-q_{s}\phi_{1}+q_{s}\rho\langle\!|\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}|\!\rangle\biggr]\mathfrak{J}_{s}({\bm{z}}^{0},t^{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}z^{0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
+\sum_{s}\int f^{0}_{s}\biggl[\frac{m_{s}}{2B_{0}^{2}}\left(\lvert\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}\rvert^{2}+2\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}\bcdot\frac{\partial{\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}}{\partial t}\right)-\frac{\mu B_{0}-m_{s}u_{\shortparallel}^{2}}{2B_{0}^{2}}\lvert{\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}\biggr]\mathfrak{J}_{00,s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}}\int\lvert{\bm{B}}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{1}\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda\bcdot{\bm{A}}_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

(5.28)

Again, a Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
is introduced which ensures well-posedness of the resulting equations.
In this case, we find that the constraint depends linearly on
\lambda
and
{\bm{A}}_{1}
, and therefore only affects Ampère’s law.
This constraint is motivated in
Section

5.3.3
.

The action (
5.28
) results in the same quasi-neutrality equation as found in
Eq.

4.33
.
The Darwin approximation of Ampère’s law is given by (cf.
Eq.

4.38
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int({\bm{B}}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{1})\bcdot\nabla\times{\bm{\Lambda}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}x={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\int\left(\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{P}}}_{1,s}^{\mathrm{Dar}}}{\partial t}+\nabla\times{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,s}\right)\bcdot{\bm{\Lambda}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}r+\int\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.56944pt\hbox{${\bm{\mathcal{J}}}$}\hskip-2.56944pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}\bcdot{\bm{\Lambda}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}r

\displaystyle+\int\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda\bcdot{\bm{\Lambda}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}x,

(5.29)

where the Darwin polarization is given by (cf.
Eqs.

4.34
and
5.7a
)

{\bm{\mathcal{P}}}_{1,s}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int f^{0}_{s}{\bm{P}}_{1,s}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathfrak{J}_{00,s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}u,\quad{\bm{P}}_{1,s}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{m_{s}}{q_{s}B_{0}^{2}}{\bm{F}}_{1,\perp}^{\mathrm{Dar}},\quad{\bm{F}}_{1,\perp}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-q_{s}\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}.

(5.30)

Setting the variation w.r.t. the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
to zero results in the following constraint

\int\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\Lambda\bcdot{\bm{A}}_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}x=0.

(5.31)

5.3.3
Well-posedness of the macroscopic Darwin equations

We find that the resulting model can be written in strong formulation as

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bm{\mathcal{H}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{D}}}^{\mathrm{Dar}}}{\partial t}+\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.56944pt\hbox{${\bm{\mathcal{J}}}$}\hskip-2.56944pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}+\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda,

(5.32a)

\displaystyle\nabla\bcdot\left({\bm{\mathcal{D}}}^{\mathrm{Dar}}-\mathcal{C}(1)\frac{\partial{\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}}{\partial t}\right)

\displaystyle=\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-3.68056pt\hbox{$\mathcal{R}$}\hskip-3.68056pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}},

(5.32b)

where the Darwin displacement field is defined as

{\bm{\mathcal{D}}}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{s}{\bm{\mathcal{P}}}_{1,s}^{\mathrm{Dar}}=-\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}.

(5.32c)

Since the Darwin displacement field explicitly depends on the potential
\phi_{1}
it follows that the Darwin model cannot directly be expressed in terms of the electromagnetic fields.

Regarding well-posedness of the resulting field equations it is important to note that the displacement field in Ampère’s law (
5.32a
) (resulting in the displacement current) does not coincide with the displacement field from the quasi-neutrality equation (
5.32b
).
However, for compatibility of the field equations, which is a necessary condition for well-posedness, we have found in
Section

4.6
that the bound continuity equation must be satisfied.
For the Darwin model the bound continuity equation is given by (cf.
Eq.

4.68
)

\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{b}}}{\partial t}+\nabla\bcdot({\bm{\mathcal{J}}}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{Dar}}+\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda)=0\quad\iff\quad\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\nabla\bcdot(\mathcal{C}(1){\bm{A}}_{1,\perp})=-\nabla\bcdot(\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda),

(5.33)

where the bound current density is given by
{\bm{\mathcal{J}}}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\partial{\bm{\mathcal{D}}}^{\mathrm{Dar}}}/{\partial t}+\sum_{s}\nabla\times{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,s}
, and we have substituted
Eqs.

4.49e
and
5.32c
.
This shows that the constraint
\nabla\bcdot(\mathcal{C}(1){\bm{A}}_{1,\perp})=0
, which in weak form is given by
Eq.

5.31
, is sufficient to ensure that the field equations are compatible, provided that we can show that the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
vanishes.

When substituting the constraint (
5.31
) into the quasi-neutrality equation (
5.32b
) we find

-\nabla\bcdot(\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1})=\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-3.68056pt\hbox{$\mathcal{R}$}\hskip-3.68056pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}

(5.34)

which is decoupled from Ampère’s law and thereby reduces to the well-known (and well-posed) perpendicular Laplace equation for
\phi_{1}
.

For the vector potential
{\bm{A}}_{1}
and Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
we have to solve the following system of equations

\displaystyle\nabla\times\left[\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\nabla\times{\bm{A}}_{1}+\frac{p_{0,\perp}-p_{0,\shortparallel}}{B_{0}^{2}}(\nabla\times{\bm{A}}_{1})_{\perp}\right]-\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda={}

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.56944pt\hbox{${\bm{\mathcal{J}}}$}\hskip-2.56944pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}-\mathcal{C}(1)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\nabla\times{\bm{B}}_{0},

(5.35a)

\displaystyle\nabla\bcdot\left(\mathcal{C}(1){\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}\right)=

\displaystyle 0,

(5.35b)

where we have substituted the definition of the magnetizing field
Eq.

4.49f
and magnetization
Eq.

4.39
(where the
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{E}}_{1}
term is no longer present due to the removal of the
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot({\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})
term from the second-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian) into
Eq.

5.32a
and the known terms are moved to the right-hand side.
The key property of
Eq.

5.35a
is that it is invariant under
{\bm{A}}_{1}\mapsto{\bm{A}}_{1}+\nabla\eta
and a gauge condition is therefore needed to fix this freedom.
This gauge condition is exactly provided by the constraint (
5.35b
).
The well-posedness of
Eq.

5.35
for an isotropic pressure is discussed in
Appendix

J
.

Note that including the
{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\bcdot({\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})
term in the second-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian would have resulted in an overdetermined system of equations for
{\bm{A}}_{1}
, and would therefore have required a non-zero
\lambda
.
It is exactly for this reason that we have omitted this term from
H_{2,s}
.

5.3.4
Energy conservation

For the Darwin model we find that evolution equation for the kinetic energy density (
4.76
) is unchanged.
The equivalent to Poynting’s theorem can also be shown, except that the potential energy density now evolves according to (cf.
Eq.

4.77
)

\frac{\partial\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{Dar}}}{\partial t}+\nabla\bcdot\left[{\bm{E}}\times{\bm{\mathcal{H}}}-\frac{\mathcal{C}(1)}{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}}{\partial t^{2}}\phi_{1}-\frac{\partial{\bm{A}}_{1,\perp}}{\partial t}\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial t}\right)\right]=-\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.56944pt\hbox{${\bm{\mathcal{J}}}$}\hskip-2.56944pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}\bcdot{\bm{E}}.

(5.36)

This results in the following conserved energy

\mathfrak{E}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int(\mathcal{T}+\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{Dar}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}r,

(5.37)

where the kinetic energy is still defined by
Eq.

4.73
and the potential energy density is given by

\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{2}({\bm{\mathcal{D}}}^{\mathrm{Dar}}\bcdot{\bm{E}}+{\bm{\mathcal{H}}}\bcdot{\bm{B}}).

(5.38)

5.4
Linearized models in a slab

In order to study the dispersive properties of the models under consideration, we consider the case of slab geometry, wherein the background magnetic field
{\bm{B}}_{0}
is assumed to be constant.
In this case the FLR corrected electromagnetic fields exactly coincide with what one would expect (cf.
Eqs.

3.60
and
4.24
)

{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}_{1}=\langle\mathring{{\bm{B}}}_{1}\rangle,\quad{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{1}=\langle\mathring{{\bm{E}}}_{1}\rangle,

(5.39)

since
\varepsilon_{B}=0
.
Furthermore, the term that multiplies
\dot{{\bm{R}}}
in Ampère’s law now exactly coincides with
\langle\mathring{{\bm{\Lambda}}}\rangle
(cf.
Eq.

D.10
).
We reiterate that each of these three identities hold only because of the specific choice of our gyrocentre coordinate transformation
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,0)
.

5.4.1
Proposed gauge invariant model

We make use of the so-called susceptibility tensor in order to study the dispersive properties of our model.
The susceptibility tensor represents the linearized model with a Fourier ansatz.
More precisely, we find that computing the time derivative of Ampère’s law (
4.49a
) results in

-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\nabla\times(\nabla\times{\bm{E}}_{1})=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\left(\epsilon_{0}{\bm{E}}_{1}+\sum_{s}{\bm{\mathcal{P}}}_{1,s}\right)+\sum_{s}\nabla\times\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,s}}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.56944pt\hbox{${\bm{\mathcal{J}}}$}\hskip-2.56944pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}}{\partial t},

(5.40)

where we have substituted
Eqs.

4.49e
,
4.49f
and
4.49b
, and note that we include a possibly non-zero vacuum permittivity
\epsilon_{0}
as this is customary when discussing the susceptibility tensor.
By substituting the expressions for the polarization (
4.34
), magnetization (
4.39
), and the gyrocentre current, and by repeatedly using Faraday’s law (
4.49b
) we can obtain an equation which is expressed entirely in terms of the perturbed electric field
{\bm{E}}_{1}
.
We then linearize this equation and substitute the Fourier ansatz

{\bm{E}}_{1}=\widehat{{\bm{E}}}_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}({\bm{k}}\bcdot{\bm{r}}-\omega t)},

(5.41)

which results in an equation of the form
(
Hasegawa 1975
, Eq. (2.37))

\omega^{2}(\mathsfbi{I}_{3}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}})\widehat{{\bm{E}}}_{1}+c^{2}{\bm{k}}\times({\bm{k}}\times\widehat{{\bm{E}}}_{1})={\bm{0}}_{3},

(5.42)

where
\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}}
is referred to as the gyrokinetic susceptibility tensor,
\mathsfbi{I}_{3}
denotes the
3\times 3
identity matrix and
c=\sqrt{1/(\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0})}
denotes the speed of light in vacuum.
Note that
\mathsfbi{I}_{3}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}}
is often referred to as the dielectric tensor.
The susceptibility tensor contains the (linearized) contributions to Ampère’s law from the polarization, magnetization as well as the gyrocentre current and reduces the linearized gyrokinetic model to a material property: the permittivity of the ‘material’ is given by
\epsilon_{0}(\mathsfbi{I}_{3}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}})
.

We follow the discussion from
(
Zonta et al. 2021
)
, wherein the gyrokinetic susceptibility tensor
\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}
is derived for the drift kinetic model from
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
(i.e. our proposed model with
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(0,0)
in the ZLR limit
\rho\rightarrow 0
) and subsequently compared to the ZLR limit
(
Hasegawa 1975
, Eq. (2.159))
of the Vlasov–Maxwell susceptibility tensor
(
Hasegawa 1975
, Eqs. (2.42) and (2.43))
.

The derivation of the susceptibility tensor can be found in
Appendix

K
, and the expression for the susceptibility tensor in its most general form can be found in
Eq.

K.32
.
We now make two simplifications: firstly, we consider the ZLR limit of the susceptibility tensor, resulting in the drift kinetic susceptibility tensor given by
Eq.

K.35
, which coincides exactly with the low-frequency and ZLR limit of the Vlasov–Maxwell susceptibility tensor found in
(
Hasegawa 1975
, Eq. (2.159))
.
And secondly, we consider the use of a centred Maxwellian background particle distribution as defined in
Eq.

5.11
.
Note that a constant background magnetic field
{\bm{B}}_{0}
combined with a centred Maxwellian background distribution with constant density trivially satisfies the equilibrium conditions discussed in
Section

4.5
.
When using the identities given by
Eq.

5.12
we find that the resulting susceptibility tensor is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{\beta_{0,s}}{u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{2}&\mathrm{i}\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}&\omega^{2}-k_{\perp}^{2}u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(5.43)

\displaystyle-\sum_{s}\frac{\beta_{0,s}}{\mathcal{N}_{00,s}u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}}\int\frac{k_{\shortparallel}^{2}f^{0,\mathrm{CM}}_{s}}{(k_{\shortparallel}u_{\shortparallel}-\omega)^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&u_{\tau}^{4}k_{\perp}^{2}\frac{1}{4}&\mathrm{i}\frac{k_{\perp}}{k_{\shortparallel}}u_{\tau}^{2}\frac{\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}}{2}\\
0&-\mathrm{i}u_{\tau}^{2}\frac{k_{\perp}}{k_{\shortparallel}}\frac{\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}}{2}&\frac{\omega^{2}\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}^{2}}{k_{\shortparallel}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}\mathfrak{J}_{00,s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}u,

where we recall that the plasma-
\beta
is defined in
Eq.

4.65
.

The remaining integrals can be expressed in terms of the plasma dispersion function
\mathcal{Z}

\mathcal{Z}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-u^{2}}}{u-\zeta}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

(5.44)

Substitution of
Eq.

5.43
in
Eq.

5.42
results in

\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{D}}}\widehat{{\bm{E}}}_{1}={\bm{0}}_{3},\quad\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{D}}}=\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\mathsfbi{I}_{3}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{X}}}+\mathsfbi{K}^{2},

(5.45)

where the matrix
\mathsfbi{K}
is such that

\mathsfbi{K}{\bm{S}}={\bm{S}}\times{\bm{k}},

(5.46)

and the Hermitian matrix
\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{D}}}
can explicitly be written as

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{D}}}={}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-k_{\shortparallel}^{2}&0&k_{\shortparallel}k_{\perp}\\
0&\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-k^{2}&0\\
k_{\shortparallel}k_{\perp}&0&\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-k_{\perp}^{2}\end{pmatrix}+\sum_{s}\frac{\beta_{0,s}}{u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{2}&\mathrm{i}\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}&\boxed{\omega^{2}}-k_{\perp}^{2}u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(5.47)

\displaystyle-\sum_{s}\frac{\beta_{0,s}}{u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}({\omega}/(k_{\shortparallel}u_{\mathrm{th},s}))\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}}{2}&\mathrm{i}\frac{k_{\perp}\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}}{2k_{\shortparallel}}\\
0&-\mathrm{i}\frac{k_{\perp}\omega\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}}{2k_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{\omega^{2}\omega_{\mathrm{c},s}^{2}}{k_{\shortparallel}^{2}u_{\mathrm{th},s}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

We highlight the boxed
{\omega^{2}}
term for the discussion that will follow in
Section

5.4.2
.

Non-trivial solutions to
Eq.

5.45
exist if and only if the dispersion matrix given by
Eq.

5.47
is singular and to this end we implicitly define the dispersion relation
\omega({\bm{k}})
via

\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{D}}}(\omega({\bm{k}}),{\bm{k}})=0.

(5.48)

5.4.2
Other models

It is a priori not clear what effect the Darwin approximation has on the dispersive properties of our model and to this end we also consider the gyrokinetic Darwin susceptibility tensor
for which a derivation can be found in
Section

K.6
.
When using a centred Maxwellian, as we did in
Section

5.4
, we find that the susceptibility tensor is given by
Eq.

5.47
where the boxed
\omega^{2}
term is removed.

As discussed in
Section

5.2
, the EOMs of the symplectic Brizard–Hahm (BH) model
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
coincide with the ones from our proposed gauge invariant model if
\varepsilon_{B}=0
, which is what we assume here.
Similarly, we find that the magnetization term vanishes, as it does in our proposed model, for the centred Maxwellian background particle distribution function that we consider here.
Hence, the only difference between the proposed model and the Brizard–Hahm model, under the current simplifying assumptions, is the missing polarization current.

However, when reconsidering
Eq.

5.24
under the current simplifying assumptions, we find that the compatibility constraint reduces to

\nabla\bcdot\left(\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\lambda\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\nabla\bcdot(\mathcal{C}(1)\nabla_{\perp}\phi_{1}),

(5.49)

upon substitution of
Eq.

5.21b
, and it therefore follows that in this case
\lambda=\partial\phi_{1}/\partial t
.
This implies that the Lagrange multiplier for the Brizard–Hahm model restores the polarization current and thereby coincides with the Darwin approximation of our model.
Note that this only works under the simplifying assumptions that we use here: the model is linearized, we assume
\varepsilon_{B}=0
, use a centred Maxwellian background distribution, and we consider the ZLR limit.

For the parallel-only model we make use of the dispersion relation provided in
(
Kleiber et al. 2016
, Eq. (40))
.

5.4.3
Comparison of the dispersion relations

The models are now compared by numerically evaluating the dispersion relations (the code can be found in
(
Remmerswaal 2023
,
examples/gauge_invariant.ipynb
)
).
We consider electron and ion (deuteron) species, with
T_{\mathrm{i}}=T_{\mathrm{e}}
,
q_{\mathrm{i}}=-q_{\mathrm{e}}
and an electron to ion mass ratio given by
m_{\mathrm{e}}/m_{\mathrm{i}}=1/3671
.
Furthermore, we nondimensionalize the frequency and wave vector as

\widecheck{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\omega}{u_{\mathrm{th},\mathrm{i}}k_{\shortparallel}},\quad\widecheck{{\bm{k}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varrho_{\mathrm{i}}{\bm{k}}.

(5.50)

In this case, we expect to find a shear and compressional Alfvén wave, which have the following frequencies (in the quasi-neutral limit
c\rightarrow\infty
)

\widecheck{\omega}_{\mathrm{As}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\beta_{0}}}},\quad\widecheck{\omega}_{\mathrm{Ac}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{k}{k_{\shortparallel}}\widecheck{\omega}_{\mathrm{As}},

(5.51)

respectively.
We note that the shear Alfvén frequency is constant w.r.t.
\widecheck{k}_{\perp}
whereas the compressional Alfvén frequency increases linearly with increasing wavenumber.
Hence, the compressional Alfvén wave is a fast wave.
This is especially true when small perpendicular length scales are considered (turbulence), since then the compressional Alfvén frequency becomes comparable to the cyclotron frequency

\varepsilon_{\perp}\sim 1,\quad\omega=\omega_{\mathrm{Ac}}\quad\implies\quad\varepsilon_{\omega}=\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{Ac}}}{\omega_{\mathrm{c},\mathrm{i}}}=\frac{\widecheck{k}}{\sqrt{\beta_{0}}}\sim 1.

(5.52)

The presence of compressional Alfvén waves in our gyrokinetic model is therefore incompatible with the low-frequency approximation of the first-order generating function (
3.54
), which relies on
\varepsilon_{\omega}\ll 1
.
This incompatibility seems to suggest that we have made a mistake in the derivation of our gyrokinetic model.
The origin of this issue lies in the low-frequency approximation of the first-order generating function as discussed in
Section

3.5.2
, where we have intentionally kept the gauge invariant parts of the right-hand side of
Eq.

3.48
.
While this ensures gauge invariance, it neglects the fact that the part of the electric field that comes from the vector potential has a time derivative, and this term would therefore be neglected if we had neglected all
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\omega})
terms.

\widecheck{{\bm{k}}}
.

\widecheck{k}_{\shortparallel}=\frac{\varrho_{\mathrm{i}}}{R_{0}},\quad\widecheck{k}_{\perp}=\frac{2\pi\varrho_{\mathrm{i}}}{a\sqrt{4+\pi^{2}}},

R_{0}

)

For each of the three models and each of the three machines, we compute the dispersion curve where we now vary
\beta_{0}
and keep the wave vectors fixed.
The results are shown in
Fig.

.

Eq.

5.51
, which suggests that the shear Alfvén frequency depends only on
\beta_{0}
.
When considering the imaginary part of the dispersion curve, we find differences not only between the machines, but also between the models, in particular for larger values of
\beta_{0}
.
The gauge invariant and Darwin model agree well, which shows that removal of the compressional Alfvén wave has not altered the shear Alfvén wave.
Moreover, we find that the parallel-only model yields a different imaginary part of the shear Alfvén frequency.

Table 2
:

Properties of the different gyrokinetic models under consideration.
The models proposed in this paper are in boldface: the gauge invariant model with
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,0)
and its corresponding Darwin approximation.

(
