)
is followed.

We have compared five different gyrokinetic models, all of which are derived from an action principle.
An overview of the most essential properties is given in
Table

2
, wherein we have also included the symplectic part of the gyrocentre single-particle phase-space Lagrangian
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1,{\bm{R}}}
– with all other components equal to zero – which defines the gyrocentre coordinate transformation.
We now summarize the comparison of the models.

The parallel-only model
(
Kleiber et al. 2016
)
, as described in
Section

5.1
, neglects the perpendicular part of the vector potential and thereby results in two scalar well-posed perpendicular Poisson-type field equations which decouple for a centred Maxwellian background particle distribution function.
The model is not gauge invariant.
Due to the absence of the perpendicular part of the vector potential, we find that the EOMs of the parallel model do not contain a grad-
B_{1,\shortparallel}
drift.
This model is still the ‘working horse’ and is widely used for the global simulation of turbulence in fusion devices
(
Garbet et al. 2010
;
Mishchenko et al. 2023
)
.

The symplectic Brizard–Hahm model
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
(see
Section

5.2
) contains the full vector potential and results in a curl-curl type Ampère’s law which is coupled to the same quasi-neutrality equation as found in the parallel-only model.
This model is also not gauge invariant and reduces to the parallel-only model when neglecting the perpendicular part of the vector potential.
The grad-
B_{1,\shortparallel}
drift is present in the EOMs; this is the case for each of the gyrokinetic models which have the full vector potential.
Interestingly, the second-order Hamiltonian of the Brizard–Hahm model coincides with that of the parallel-only model.
This implies that this model has no polarization current, and the magnetization effects do not depend on the perpendicular part of the vector potential.
Moreover, the magnetization current is not the curl of a magnetization, and we find that the continuity equation is not satisfied for the gyro-average adjoint of the free charge and current density.
For those reasons the total continuity equation, consisting of the free and bound charge, is not satisfied, which is a necessary condition for well-posedness of the field equations.
It follows that well-posedness of the Brizard–Hahm model
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
requires the introduction of a Lagrange multiplier, which affects both the EOMs and the field equations in an unphysical way.

The gyrokinetic model from
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
has played an essential role in this paper, as it has guided us to the development of a family of gauge invariant gyrokinetic models.
The model from
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
coincides with
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(0,0)
, except that the authors propose to neglect the polarization current altogether, as it is a higher-order contribution in
\varepsilon_{\omega}
.
This decision however does imply that a Lagrange multiplier will be needed for this model to make it well-posed.
We have not discussed this model in any detail beyond the computation of the first- and second-order Hamiltonian, as found in
Eqs.

3.47
and
3.57
.
The reason that we have not considered this parameter combination any further is because
\xi_{R}=0
results in a model where the symplectic part of the model is essentially drift-kinetic, does not reduce to the parallel-only model if the perpendicular part of the vector potential is neglected, and the Hamiltonian part of the model contains terms which close themselves off a physical interpretation.

Instead, we propose to use
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,0)
, which results in a gauge invariant gyrokinetic model for which all terms in both the EOMs and the field equations can be interpreted from a physical point of view.
Moreover, it yields the smallest coordinate transformation while resulting in a gauge invariant model wherein the gyrocentre magnetic moment is an invariant.
This model does reduce to the parallel-only model if the perpendicular part of the vector potential is neglected.
The single-particle Lagrangian and resulting Vlasov–Maxwell action principle of this model are derived and described in detail in
Sections

3
and
4
, respectively.
We find that the continuity equation is satisfied for the gyro-average adjoint of the free charge and current density, and, since the model is gauge invariant and derived from an action principle, it follows that the bound charge continuity equation is satisfied as well.
These are necessary conditions for well-posedness of the field equations, which are satisfied without the need of a Lagrange multiplier.

The presence of the polarization current in our proposed gauge invariant model results in a compressional Alfvén wave, which is often undesired due to its relatively high frequency.
To this end, we have proposed a Darwin approximation to our gauge invariant gyrokinetic model, which is discussed in
Section

5.3
.
Therein, we deliberately remove the part of the polarization current which is responsible for the compressional Alfvén wave, but we thereby break gauge invariance of the model.
However, we have shown that the field equations are well-posed and decouple without the need of a Lagrange multiplier.

Finally, we have derived a dispersion relation for each of the models, which has confirmed the expected properties of the models: each model has the shear Alfvén wave and only the proposed gauge invariant model includes the compressional Alfvén wave.
In both cases the real part of the frequency agrees well with the theory, whereas the imaginary part is negative and thereby results in damping of the wave.

6
Conclusions

Motivated by the need for a more complete gyrokinetic model, wherein the perpendicular part of the vector potential is kept, we have discussed the gyrocentre coordinate transformation in detail.
The purpose of the gyrocentre coordinate transformation is to transform the perturbed single-particle guiding-centre phase-space Lagrange in such a way that it becomes independent of the gyro-phase.
This results in a reduction of phase-space dimension from six to five (the gyrocentre position, the parallel velocity and the invariant magnetic moment), and when moreover considering the limit of quasi-neutrality, removes the fastest time scales from the large range of length and time scales present in the Vlasov–Maxwell model.

When gyrokinetic modelling is considered at the level of the Lagrangian, which thereby utilizes a variational principle, it is ensured that the gyrokinetic model is structure preserving.
In particular, we find that energy is conserved regardless of the modelling error introduced by the truncated gyrocentre coordinate transformation.
However, an aspect that is often overlooked in gyrokinetics is the property of gauge invariance
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
, which ensures that the model is invariant under the gauge transformation, as is the case of the Vlasov–Maxwell model.
In particular, the traditionally used parallel-only models are not gauge invariant.
To this end, we have generalized the approach proposed in
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
, wherein a gauge invariant gyrokinetic model is introduced.
We have derived sufficient conditions on the gyrocentre coordinate transformation which ensure that the resulting single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian is gauge invariant.
Despite this additional restriction, the gyrocentre coordinate transformation is by no means uniquely defined, and this approach therefore results in a family of gauge invariant gyrokinetic models.
The family of models is parametrized by the two parameters
\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta}
, where the model from
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
coincides with
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(0,0)
.
Our derivation is presented without making use of the formalism of differential geometry.

In an effort to obtain a gyrokinetic model for which each of the equations of motion as well as the field equations can be interpreted from a physical point of view, we have chosen
(\xi_{R},\xi_{\Theta})=(1,0)
.
This choice leads to the smallest coordinate transformation which results in a gauge invariant model wherein the gyrocentre magnetic moment is an invariant.
We find that our model reduces to the parallel-only model when the perpendicular component of the vector potential is neglected, which does not hold for the gauge invariant model from
(
Burby & Brizard 2019
)
.
The resulting model has been derived to second-order in the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon_{\delta}
, and the second-order part of the Lagrangian contains polarization and magnetization effects which have a clear physical meaning.
Due to gauge invariance the model can be expressed directly in terms of the electromagnetic fields, rather than the potentials.
The gyrokinetic model thereby results in the macroscopic Maxwell’s equations.
Moreover, we have derived equilibrium conditions on the background distribution function and magnetic field which justify the smallness of the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon_{\delta}
.

We find that our gauge invariant model possesses a magnetization current which is the curl of a magnetization.
In addition, it has a polarization current, and we find that our free charge continuity equation holds for the gyro-average adjoints of the free charge and current.
Each of those three properties is essential for showing that the bound charge continuity equation is naturally satisfied, which is necessary for well-posedness of the field equations.
Hence, unlike the Brizard–Hahm model
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
)
, we find that the field equations of our gauge invariant model are compatible without the need of a Lagrange multiplier.
A brief summary of the comparison between each of the models under consideration is found in
Table

2
.

In addition, we have derived the gyrokinetic susceptibility tensor, which covers the material properties of the linearized gyrokinetic model in the macroscopic Maxwell’s equations, for each of the models under consideration.
In the ZLR limit, we find that the gyrokinetic susceptibility tensor of our gauge invariant model agrees with that of the Vlasov–Maxwell system.
Moreover, the resulting dispersion relation shows that, besides the usual shear Alfvén wave, a fast compressional Alfvén wave is present in our gauge invariant model.
In fact, the compressional Alfvén wave is necessarily present when gauge invariance is sought after, due to the need for a gauge invariant approximation of the first-order generating function.
Due to the potentially high frequency of this wave we have proposed a Darwin approximation to our proposed gauge invariant model which successfully removes the fast compressional Alfvén wave without breaking the structure of the field equations.
We find that our Darwin model is still well-posed without the need of a Lagrange multiplier.

In future work, we plan to implement the two proposed models in the gyrokinetic particle-in-cell code EUTERPE
(
Jost et al. 2001
;
Kleiber et al. 2024
)
and compare them to the well-established and traditionally used parallel-only model.

Appendix A
Inversion of the Lagrange matrix

We consider the inversion of the Lagrange matrix

\mathsfbi{W}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsfbi{W}_{11}&\mathsfbi{W}_{12}\\
-\mathsfbi{W}_{12}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&\mathsfbi{W}_{22}\end{pmatrix},

(A.1)

where

\mathsfbi{W}_{11}=\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}&-m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\\
m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsfbi{W}_{12}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{m}{q}{\bm{w}}&{\bm{0}}_{3}\\
0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsfbi{W}_{22}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{m}{q}\\
-\frac{m}{q}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(A.2)

Here
\mathsfbi{B}
is such that

\mathsfbi{B}{\bm{S}}={\bm{S}}\times{\bm{B}}

(A.3)

for some magnetic field
{\bm{B}}
.

The inverse of
\mathsfbi{W}_{22}
is readily given by

\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{q}{m}\\
\frac{q}{m}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(A.4)

Using the Schur complement

\mathsfbi{S}=\mathsfbi{W}_{11}+\mathsfbi{W}_{12}\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}},

(A.5)

we find that the inverse of the
2\times 2
block matrix
\mathsfbi{W}
is given by

\mathsfbi{W}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}&-\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}\\
\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}&\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}-\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.6)

The expressions for the block matrices result in

\mathsfbi{S}=\mathsfbi{W}_{11}

(A.7)

for which the inverse is given by

\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathsfbi{B}_{0}}{qB_{0}B_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{{\bm{B}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}\\
-\frac{{\bm{B}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}&0\end{pmatrix}

(A.8)

as can be verified by computing the product

\mathsfbi{S}\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}&-m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\\
m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathsfbi{B}_{0}}{qB_{0}B_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{{\bm{B}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}\\
-\frac{{\bm{B}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{-\mathsfbi{B}\mathsfbi{B}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{0}{\bm{B}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{B_{0}B_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{q\mathsfbi{B}{\bm{B}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}\\
-\frac{m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\mathsfbi{B}_{0}}{qB_{0}B_{\shortparallel}}&1\end{pmatrix}

(A.9)

and observing that using
Eq.

2.36

\mathsfbi{B}{\bm{B}}={\bm{B}}\times{\bm{B}}={\bm{0}},\quad\mathsfbi{B}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}={\bm{B}}_{0}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}={\bm{0}}

(A.10)

as well as

\displaystyle\left(-\mathsfbi{B}\mathsfbi{B}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{0}{\bm{B}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\right){\bm{S}}

\displaystyle=-\mathsfbi{B}({\bm{S}}\times{\bm{B}}_{0})+{\bm{B}}_{0}({\bm{B}}\bcdot{\bm{S}})

\displaystyle=-({\bm{S}}\times{\bm{B}}_{0})\times{\bm{B}}+{\bm{B}}_{0}({\bm{B}}\bcdot{\bm{S}})

\displaystyle=B_{\shortparallel}B_{0}{\bm{S}}

(A.11)

for any vector
{\bm{S}}
, which implies that
-\mathsfbi{B}\mathsfbi{B}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{0}{\bm{B}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}=B_{\shortparallel}B_{0}\mathsfbi{I}_{3}
.

Using these intermediate results we are ready to evaluate the blocks of the inverse of
\mathsfbi{W}

(\mathsfbi{W}^{-1})_{12}=-\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{0}}_{3}&-\frac{{\bm{w}}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}}{qB_{\shortparallel}}\\
0&-\frac{{\bm{B}}\bcdot{\bm{w}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(A.12)

and

(\mathsfbi{W}^{-1})_{22}=\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}-\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\mathsfbi{S}^{-1}\mathsfbi{W}_{12}\mathsfbi{W}_{22}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{q}{m}\\
\frac{q}{m}&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(A.13)

such that the inverse of the Lagrange matrix is given by

\mathsfbi{W}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathsfbi{B}_{0}}{qB_{0}B_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{{\bm{B}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}&{\bm{0}}_{3}&-\frac{{\bm{w}}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}}{qB_{\shortparallel}}\\
-\frac{{\bm{B}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}&0&0&-\frac{{\bm{B}}\bcdot{\bm{w}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&-\frac{q}{m}\\
\frac{({\bm{w}}\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}{qB_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{{\bm{B}}\bcdot{\bm{w}}}{mB_{\shortparallel}}&\frac{q}{m}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(A.14)

Appendix B
Gyrocentre coordinate transformation

The gyrocentre Lagrangian
\bar{\bar{L}}
is defined according to
Eq.

3.6
.
The transformation rules for the Hamiltonian and symplectic part follow directly by substituting
Eq.

3.5
into
Eq.

3.6
and by subsequently making use of a Taylor series expansion centred around the gyrocentre coordinate
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
.
The contribution due to the generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}
is omitted here.

For the guiding-centre Hamiltonian this results in

\bar{H}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}})=\bar{H}+\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\left(-\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}-\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{H}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}^{2}}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\otimes\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}^{3}),

(B.1)

upon substitution of
Eq.

3.5
and using a Taylor-series expansion of
\bar{H}
centred around the gyrocentre coordinate
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
.
We use the notational convention that an absence of arguments implies evaluation at the gyrocentre coordinate, and we let
\otimes
denote the outer product of two vectors

({\bm{a}}\otimes{\bm{b}})_{ij}=a_{i}b_{j},

(B.2a)

such that

\mathsfbi{M}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}{\bm{a}}\otimes{\bm{b}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\mathsfi{M}_{ij}a_{i}b_{j}.

(B.2b)

This expression can be simplified to

\bar{H}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}})=\bar{H}-\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}^{3}).

(B.3)

The same approach is followed for the symplectic part of the guiding-centre Lagrangian.
Let
\bar{\Gamma}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}})=\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}})\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
.
Substitution of
Eq.

3.5
results in

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}(\bar{{\bm{Z}}})={}

\displaystyle\left[\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}^{2}}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\otimes\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\bcdot\left[\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\delta}^{3}),

(B.4)

where we write
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}
for brevity, and we use the notational convention that an absence of arguments implies evaluation at the gyrocentre coordinate
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
.
We are permitted to add a total derivative, by virtue of considering an action principle; we add the total derivative of

\left(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}\right)\bcdot\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right),

(B.5)

resulting in the following symplectic part of the gyrocentre Lagrangian

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}={}

\displaystyle\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}+\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}^{2}}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\otimes\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left[\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right]^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\right]^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\right)\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1},

(B.6)

where the following piece of the symplectic part of the transformed guiding-centre Lagrangian moves to the Hamiltonian part of the gyrocentre Lagrangian (while switching sign)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1},

(B.7)

where we have made use of
\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}/\partial t={\bm{0}}_{6}
.

To further simplify
Eq.

B.6
we now write it component wise as

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{{\gamma}}}_{i}={}

\displaystyle\bar{{\gamma}}_{i}+\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{ij}\left(\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{Z}}}_{k}}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,k}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{{\gamma}}_{i}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{Z}}}_{j}\partial\bar{\bar{{Z}}}_{k}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{{\gamma}}_{0,j}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{Z}}}_{i}\partial\bar{\bar{{Z}}}_{k}}\right)\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,k}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{kj}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{Z}}}_{i}}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,k},

(B.8)

where we have simplified the expression, and we note that a repeated index implies summation.
Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{i}}\left(\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{kj}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{k}}\left(\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{ij}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}\right)={}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{\gamma}_{i}}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{j}\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{k}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{\gamma}_{k}}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{j}\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{i}}\right)\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}

\displaystyle+\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{kj}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{i}}-\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{ij}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{k}},

(B.9)

which allows
Eq.

B.8
to be written as

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{{\gamma}}}_{i}={}

\displaystyle\bar{{\gamma}}_{i}+\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{ij}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{j}+\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{i}}\left(\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{kj}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{Z}}_{k}}\left(\bar{\mathsfi{W}}_{ij}\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,j}\right)\right]\bar{\bar{{G}}}_{1,k}.

(B.10)

Finally, the gyrocentre Hamiltonian follows from subtracting
Eq.

B.7
from
Eq.

B.3
resulting in

\bar{\bar{H}}=\bar{H}-\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{2}\right)+\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\right]\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}.

(B.11)

Appendix C
Sufficient condition for gauge invariance

Theorem 1
(Sufficient condition for gauge invariance)

The single-particle gyrocentre phase-space Lagrangian (to second order) is gauge invariant provided that
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
are gauge invariant.

Proof C.2
.

We assume that
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}
is gauge invariant.
Note that the perturbed guiding-centre Lagrangian (
3.34
) is gauge invariant (up to a total derivative)

\bar{L}_{1}\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:gauge_transform}}}{\mapsto}\bar{L}_{1}+q\left(\nabla\eta\bcdot\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial t}\right)=\bar{L}_{1}+q\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}t}.

(C.1)

From
Eqs.

3.10b
and
3.9b
it follows that the first-order gyrocentre Lagrangian can be written as

\bar{\bar{L}}_{1}=\bar{L}_{1}+\left(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{0}\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}+\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}+\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t},

(C.2)

from which it follows that
\bar{\bar{L}}_{1}
is gauge invariant if the first-order generating vector
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
and first-order generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
are gauge invariant.

Using
Eqs.

3.14
,
3.15
and
3.34c
we find that

\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}}{\partial t}+\{\bar{\bar{S}}_{1},\bar{\bar{H}}_{0}\}_{0}=q\widetilde{\psi}_{1}=\widetilde{\bar{H}_{1}}+\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\bcdot\widetilde{\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\right)}=\widetilde{\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}}+\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}\bcdot\widetilde{\left(\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}\right)},

(C.3)

where we note that
\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{FLR}}
is gauge invariant as it is expressed in terms of the electric field, and therefore
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}
is gauge invariant since
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1}-\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}
is assumed to be gauge invariant.
It follows that the generating vector
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
, by
Eq.

3.12
, and thereby also the first-order gyrocentre Lagrangian are gauge invariant.

We additionally assume that
\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}
is gauge invariant.
The second-order gyrocentre Lagrangian is given by

\bar{\bar{L}}_{2}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{2}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\bar{\bar{H}}_{2},

(C.4)

where the second-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian is given by
Eq.

3.23
.
In order for the second-order gyrocentre Lagrangian to be gauge invariant, we therefore need to show that the corresponding Hamiltonian is gauge invariant, which, upon inspection of
Eq.

3.23
, requires the vector
{\bm{T}}_{1}

to be gauge invariant.

The vector
{\bm{T}}_{1}
, as given by
Eq.

3.21
, can be written as

{\bm{T}}_{1}=\underbrace{\left[\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{1}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right]}_{{\bm{T}}_{1}^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}}{\partial t}+(\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1}-\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1})\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)\right],

(C.5)

where we note that
\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1}-\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}
is gauge invariant because
\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}
is gauge invariant (see
Eq.

3.11
).
It therefore remains to be shown that
{\bm{T}}_{1}^{\dagger}
is gauge invariant, which is expressed in terms of the first-order symplectic and Hamiltonian part of guiding-centre Lagrangian.
We now note that the FLR part of the first-order guiding-centre Lagrangian is expressed in terms of the electromagnetic fields (see
Eq.

3.34
) and is therefore gauge invariant.
Hence, we need only to show that the ZLR part
{\bm{T}}_{1}^{\dagger,\mathrm{ZLR}}
is gauge invariant, which is given by

\displaystyle{\bm{T}}_{1}^{\dagger,\mathrm{ZLR}}=\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{ZLR}}}{\partial t}=\begin{pmatrix}q({\bm{E}}_{1}+\dot{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(C.6)

and is therefore gauge invariant.

Appendix D
Gyro-averaging identities

D.1
Taylor-series expansions

Let
Q=Q({\bm{r}},t)
be a smooth function.
We denote by
\mathsfbi{H}Q
the Hessian matrix of second-order partial derivatives of
Q

(\mathsfbi{H}Q)_{ij}=\frac{\partial^{2}Q}{\partial r_{i}\partial r_{j}},

(D.1)

which allows us to write the Taylor series expansion of
Q
centred around
{\bm{r}}
as

\mathring{Q}=Q({\bm{r}}+\rho{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})=Q+\rho{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\bcdot\nabla Q+\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\mathsfbi{H}Q\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\otimes{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{3}).

(D.2)

Where we recall
Eq.

B.2
, and note that an absence of arguments implies evaluation at
({\bm{r}},t)
.

Nondimensionalization of
Eq.

D.2
results in

\widecheck{Q}({\bm{r}}+\rho{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})=\widecheck{Q}+\varepsilon_{\perp}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\bcdot\nabla\widecheck{Q}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\perp}^{2}}{2}\widecheck{\mathsfbi{H}}\widecheck{Q}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\otimes{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp}^{3}),

(D.3)

where
Q=[Q]\widecheck{Q}
, and we recall that the nondimensional perpendicular wavenumber is defined in
Eq.

2.18
.

We note that the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp}^{3})
term in
Eq.

D.3
contains a
{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\otimes{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\otimes{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}
term, which yields zero upon computing the gyro-average.
It follows that the gyro-average of
\mathring{Q}
has the following Taylor series expansion

\langle\mathring{Q}\rangle=Q+\frac{\rho^{2}}{4}\mathsfbi{H}Q\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}({\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}\otimes{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}+{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}\otimes{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\perp}^{4}),

(D.4)

where we have multiplied
Eq.

D.3
by
[Q]
and have made use of

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\rangle

\displaystyle={\bm{0}}_{3},

(D.5a)

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\otimes{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}\otimes{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}+{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}\otimes{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}}{2}.

(D.5b)

D.2
Stokes’ theorem

Using the definition of the gyro-average, as found in
Eq.

3.16
, we find that

\langle\mathring{S}_{\tau}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\bm{S}}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+{\bm{\rho}})\bcdot{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}},\bar{\bar{\theta}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{\theta}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\rho}\int_{\partial D_{\rho}}{\bm{S}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{t}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}l,

(D.6)

where
{\bm{\hat{t}}}\perp{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}
is the counter-clockwise tangent to the boundary of the disk
D_{\rho}
centred at
\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}
(i.e. the shaded disk shown in
Fig.

1
), which results in the minus sign.
Using Stokes’ theorem and
Eq.

3.44
, we then find that

\langle\mathring{S}_{\tau}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\rho}\int_{D_{\rho}}(\nabla\times{\bm{S}})_{\shortparallel}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}x=-\frac{\rho}{2}\frac{1}{\pi\rho^{2}}\int_{D_{\rho}}(\nabla\times{\bm{S}})_{\shortparallel}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}x=-\frac{\rho}{2}\langle\!\langle\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{S}}\rangle\!\rangle_{\shortparallel},

(D.7)

where
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}x
is an infinitesimal area element on the disk.

D.3
The gradient theorem

Terms containing a radial average (i.e. an integral over
\varsigma
) can often be interpreted in some alternative way.
For instance, the gradient theorem (or fundamental theorem of calculus for line integrals) results in

\rho\langle\!|{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\bcdot\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}Q|\!\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}{\bm{\rho}}\bcdot(\nabla Q)(\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}+\varsigma{\bm{\rho}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{\bar{\theta}}=\langle\mathring{Q}\rangle-Q.

(D.8)

The same identity can be derived for vector fields

\rho\langle\!|\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}{\bm{S}}{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}|\!\rangle=\langle\mathring{{\bm{S}}}\rangle-{\bm{S}}.

(D.9)

By making use of
\nabla({\bm{a}}\bcdot{\bm{b}})={\bm{a}}\times(\nabla\times{\bm{b}})+{\bm{b}}\times(\nabla\times{\bm{a}})+(\nabla{\bm{b}}){\bm{a}}+(\nabla{\bm{a}}){\bm{b}}
, while neglecting
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contributions, we then find that

\langle\!|\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}(\rho S_{\rho})|\!\rangle+\rho\langle\!|(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{S}})\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}|\!\rangle+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})=\langle\mathring{{\bm{S}}}\rangle-{\bm{S}}.

(D.10)

Similarly, we find that application of
Eq.

D.9
to the curl of a vector field results in

\rho\langle\!|\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}(\nabla\times{\bm{S}}){\bm{\hat{\rho}}}|\!\rangle=\langle\mathring{\nabla}\times{\bm{S}}\rangle-\nabla\times{\bm{S}}.

(D.11)

By making use of
\nabla\times({\bm{a}}\times{\bm{b}})={\bm{a}}\nabla\bcdot{\bm{b}}-{\bm{b}}\nabla\bcdot{\bm{a}}+(\nabla{\bm{a}}){\bm{b}}-(\nabla{\bm{b}}){\bm{a}}
, while neglecting
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contributions, we then find that

\rho\langle\!|\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times[(\nabla\times{\bm{S}})\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}]|\!\rangle+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})=\langle\mathring{\nabla}\times{\bm{S}}\rangle-\nabla\times{\bm{S}}.

(D.12)

Appendix E
Approximation of the second-order Hamiltonian

We consider
Eq.

3.56
, with the first-order generating vector approximated by
Eq.

3.55
and
{\bm{T}}_{1}
is as defined by
Eq.

3.21
, which we repeat here for convenience

{\bm{T}}_{1}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1})\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}_{\hbox to10.27pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.1355pt\lower-5.1355pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\left(\bar{H}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{H}_{0}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\bar{\bar{{\bm{G}}}}_{1}\right)}_{\hbox to10.12pt{\vbox to10.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-5.05933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.65 0 C 6.65 3.68 3.68 6.65 0 6.65 C -3.68 6.65 -6.65 3.68 -6.65 0 C -6.65 -3.68 -3.68 -6.65 0 -6.65 C 3.68 -6.65 6.65 -3.68 6.65 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.06949pt}{-2.73332pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(E.1)

In this section of the appendix, we use the following shorthand notation

Q^{\xi_{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{2}(Q+\xi_{R}\langle Q\rangle).

(E.2)

For the approximation of
\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}
we omit all derivatives of the background magnetic field as well as of the perturbed electromagnetic fields.

Using
Eqs.

3.34b
and
3.42
we find that

\frac{1}{2}(\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}q{\bm{A}}_{1}+q\rho\int_{0}^{1}(\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})^{\xi_{R}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\\
0\\
0\\
-\frac{q\rho^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\varsigma(\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\\
\end{pmatrix},

(E.3)

from which it follows that

\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1})=\int_{0}^{1}\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}_{1}&{\bm{0}}_{3}&-\frac{q\rho}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}(\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})^{\xi_{R}}&-\frac{q\rho}{2}\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\\
{\bm{0}}_{3}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&0\\
\frac{q\rho}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}\left[(\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})^{\xi_{R}}\right]^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&0&-\frac{q\rho^{2}}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}\varsigma(\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel})\\
\frac{q\rho}{2}\left(\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0&\frac{q\rho^{2}}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}\varsigma(\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel})&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(E.4)

where the matrix
\mathsfbi{B}_{1}
is defined analogously to
Eq.

2.36
.
We have omitted all derivatives of the background magnetic field as well as of the perturbed electromagnetic fields.
Subsequent evaluation of the matrix-vector product with
\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
(while neglecting
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
contributions), where the guiding-centre EOMs are given by
Eq.

2.42
, results in

\hbox to10.27pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.1355pt\lower-5.1355pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.76 0 C 6.76 3.73 3.73 6.76 0 6.76 C -3.73 6.76 -6.76 3.73 -6.76 0 C -6.76 -3.73 -3.73 -6.76 0 -6.76 C 3.73 -6.76 6.76 -3.73 6.76 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.18338pt}{-2.73332pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{1})\dot{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}=\int_{0}^{1}\begin{pmatrix}q\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{B}}_{1}-\frac{q\rho\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}{2}\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\\
0\\
\frac{q\rho\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}(\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}})^{\xi_{R}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}-\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{c}}q\rho^{2}}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}\varsigma(\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel})\\
\frac{q\rho\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}{2}(\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}})\bcdot{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma.

(E.5)

The second term in
{\bm{T}}_{1}
is given by

\hbox to10.05pt{\vbox to10.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-5.02734pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.61 0 C 6.61 3.65 3.65 6.61 0 6.61 C -3.65 6.61 -6.61 3.65 -6.61 0 C -6.61 -3.65 -3.65 -6.61 0 -6.61 C 3.65 -6.61 6.61 -3.65 6.61 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.00836pt}{-2.73332pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\begin{pmatrix}q\frac{\partial{\bm{A}}_{1}}{\partial t}\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(E.6)

by making use of
Eqs.

3.34b
,
2.21
and
3.55
as well as Faraday’s equation.
The third term in
{\bm{T}}_{1}
involves the average of the first-order guiding-centre and gyrocentre Hamiltonians

\frac{1}{2}(\bar{H}_{1}+\bar{\bar{H}}_{1})=q\phi_{1}-\int_{0}^{1}\left[\frac{q\rho}{2}(\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}+\langle\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}\rangle)+(1-\xi_{R})\frac{q\rho}{2}\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\rangle-\bar{\bar{M}}\varsigma\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(E.7)

which follows from substituting
Eqs.

3.34c
and
3.47
.
This results in

\hbox to10.12pt{\vbox to10.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-5.05933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.65 0 C 6.65 3.68 3.68 6.65 0 6.65 C -3.68 6.65 -6.65 3.68 -6.65 0 C -6.65 -3.68 -3.68 -6.65 0 -6.65 C 3.68 -6.65 6.65 -3.68 6.65 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.06949pt}{-2.73332pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\int_{0}^{1}\begin{pmatrix}q\nabla\phi_{1}\\
-\frac{q\rho(1-\xi_{R})}{2}\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\rangle\\
-\frac{q\rho}{4\bar{\bar{M}}}(\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}+\langle\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}\rangle)-(1-\xi_{R})\frac{q\rho\bar{U}_{\shortparallel}}{4\bar{\bar{M}}}\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\rangle+\varsigma\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\\
-\frac{q\rho}{2}\mathring{E}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(E.8)

where we have again neglected all derivatives of the background and perturbed electromagnetic fields.

When combining
Eqs.

E.5
,
E.6
and
E.8
we find that
{\bm{T}}_{1}
is given by

{\bm{T}}_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{F}}_{1}-\frac{q\rho\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}{2}\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\\
\frac{q\rho(1-\xi_{R})}{2}\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\rangle\\
\frac{\rho}{4\bar{\bar{M}}}(\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}+\langle\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}\rangle)-\varsigma(\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}+\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle)\\
\frac{\rho}{2}\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma,

(E.9)

where we have made use of the definition of the Lorentz force
{\bm{F}}_{1}
as given by
Eq.

3.51
.
We are now ready to evaluate the second-order gyrocentre Hamiltonian, as expressed in
Eq.

3.56
, by substituting
Eqs.

3.55
and
E.9

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[{\bm{F}}_{1}-\frac{q\rho\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\right]\bcdot\left[\frac{{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times}{B_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\xi_{R}\langle\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}\rangle-\mathring{{\bm{B}}}^{\varsigma}_{1}\times{\bm{\rho}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma+\frac{\rho B_{1,\rho}}{B_{0}}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\left\langle\left[\frac{q\rho(1-\xi_{R})}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\right]\left[-\frac{q\rho}{m}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\xi_{R}\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\rangle-\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\left\langle\int_{0}^{1}\left[\frac{\rho}{4\bar{\bar{M}}}(\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}+\langle\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\rho}\rangle)-\varsigma(\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}+\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle)\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\left[\frac{q^{2}\rho^{2}}{m}\int_{0}^{1}\varsigma\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma-\frac{\rho}{B_{0}}F_{1,\rho}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\left\langle\left[\frac{\rho}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathring{F}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varsigma\right]\left[-\frac{\rho}{2B_{0}\bar{\bar{M}}}F_{1,\tau}\right]\right\rangle.

(E.10)

We consider the ZLR limit of all the terms, except for the term which moves to the particle part of the Hamiltonian, as discussed in
Section

4.2
.
That is, we make use of the approximation
\mathring{Q}^{\varsigma}\approx Q
resulting in

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\rho}{B_{0}}\left\langle\left[{\bm{F}}_{1}-\frac{q\rho\omega_{\mathrm{c}}}{2}{{\bm{B}}}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\right]\bcdot\left[{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\left(\xi_{R}\langle{{\bm{B}}}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\rangle-{{\bm{B}}}_{1}\times{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\right)+B_{1,\rho}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\right]\right\rangle

(E.11)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\left[\frac{\rho}{2\bar{\bar{M}}}({F}_{1,\rho}+\langle{F}_{1,\rho}\rangle)-{B}_{1,\shortparallel}\right]\left[-\frac{q^{2}\rho^{2}}{2m}{B}_{1,\shortparallel}-\frac{\rho}{B_{0}}F_{1,\rho}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\frac{\rho^{2}}{4B_{0}\bar{\bar{M}}}\left\langle F_{1,\tau}^{2}\right\rangle+(1-\xi_{R})^{2}\frac{q^{2}\rho^{2}}{2m}\langle\!|\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}|\!\rangle^{2},

where we have moreover approximated
B_{0,\shortparallel}^{\star}\approx B_{0}
.
When subsequently making use of
\langle{\bm{\hat{\tau}}}\rangle=\langle{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\rangle={\bm{0}}_{3}
as well as

\langle S_{\rho}^{2}\rangle=\langle S_{\tau}^{2}\rangle={\bm{S}}\otimes{\bm{S}}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}\langle{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\otimes{\bm{\hat{\rho}}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}{\bm{S}}\otimes{\bm{S}}\mathbin{\vbox{{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}\vskip-10.0pt{\hbox{$\bcdot$}}}}({\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}\otimes{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1}+{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}\otimes{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\lvert{\bm{S}}_{\perp}\rvert^{2},

(E.12)

we find that

\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}=\frac{\bar{\bar{M}}}{2B_{0}}\lvert{\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}-\frac{m}{2q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}\lvert{\bm{F}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}+(1-\xi_{R})^{2}\frac{q^{2}\rho^{2}}{2m}\langle\!|\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\tau}|\!\rangle^{2}.

(E.13)

Appendix F
Computation of the Jacobian

The aim is to compute the Jacobian of the transformation from the physical coordinates
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=({\bm{R}},{\bm{U}})
to the gyrocentre coordinates
\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}
.
To this end, we write the Lagrangian in physical coordinates
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
as

\tilde{L}=\tilde{{\bm{\gamma}}}\bcdot\dot{\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}-\tilde{H}.

(F.1)

Here the Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}
is defined such that, when the physical Lagrangian is transformed to gyrocentre coordinates, we find

\tilde{L}(\tilde{{\bm{Z}}})=\bar{\bar{L}}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}).

(F.2)

Letting
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}})
, we find that

\dot{\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}=\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}},

(F.3)

such that
Eqs.

F.1
and
F.2
imply

\tilde{{\bm{\gamma}}}\bcdot\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)=\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}=\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\tilde{{\bm{\gamma}}}.

(F.4)

This results in the following expression for the gyrocentre Lagrange matrix

\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}=\left(\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{\gamma}}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}-\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{\gamma}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\right)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\tilde{\mathsfbi{W}}\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}},

(F.5)

from which it follows that

\det\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}}{\det\tilde{\mathsfbi{W}}}}.

(F.6)

We note that

\det\tilde{\mathsfbi{W}}=\det\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}_{0}&-m\mathsfbi{I}_{3}\\
m\mathsfbi{I}_{3}&\mathsfbi{0}_{3}\\
\end{pmatrix}=m^{6}

(F.7)

and

\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}=\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{S}}}\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{22}=\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{11}\frac{m^{2}}{q^{2}}

(F.8)

by the Schur complement determinant formula, where we use the block structure of the Lagrange matrix as discussed in
Appendix

A
.
Finally, we note that direct computation shows that

\det\bar{\bar{\mathsfbi{W}}}_{11}=\det\begin{pmatrix}q\mathsfbi{B}^{\star}&-m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\\
m{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}&0\end{pmatrix}=(qmB_{\shortparallel}^{\star})^{2},

(F.9)

such that

\det\frac{\partial\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\frac{B_{\shortparallel}^{\star}}{m}.

(F.10)

Appendix G
Proof of Liouville’s theorem

Theorem G.3

(Gyrocenter Liouville theorem)
.

Phase-space volume is conserved

\frac{\partial\mathfrak{J}_{s}}{\partial t}+\nabla\bcdot(\mathfrak{J}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}})+\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}}(\mathfrak{J}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{U}}}_{\shortparallel})=0.

(G.1)

Furthermore, integrals of the form
Eq.

4.4
can be expressed in terms of the initial phase-space coordinates in the following way

\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}\bar{\bar{z}}=\int\bar{\bar{f}}^{0}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0})\mathcal{F}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}(t;\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0}))\mathfrak{J}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}\bar{\bar{z}}^{0},

(G.2)

where an absence of arguments implies evaluation at
\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}},t
.

Proof G.4
.

The gyrocentre EOMs (
3.69
) imply that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial B_{s,\shortparallel}^{\star}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\mathcal{B}_{1,\shortparallel}}{\partial t},

(G.3a)

\displaystyle\nabla\bcdot(B_{s,\shortparallel}^{\star}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bm{B}}_{s}^{\star}}{m_{s}}\bcdot\nabla\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{H}}_{s}}{\partial\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}+\frac{1}{q_{s}}\left(\nabla\bar{\bar{H}}_{s}+q_{s}\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right)\bcdot(\nabla\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})-\frac{\partial\mathcal{B}_{1,\shortparallel}}{\partial t},

(G.3b)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}(B_{s,\shortparallel}^{\star}\dot{\bar{\bar{U}}}_{\shortparallel})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{q_{s}}(\nabla\times{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0})\bcdot\left(\nabla\bar{\bar{H}}_{s}+q_{s}\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right)-\frac{{\bm{B}}_{s}^{\star}}{m_{s}}\bcdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{U}}_{\shortparallel}}\nabla\bar{\bar{H}}_{s},

(G.3c)

from which
Eq.

G.1
follows by simply adding the contributions.

We consider the coordinate transformation
\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}=\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}(t;\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0})\mapsto\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0}
applied to the left-hand side of
Eq.

G.2
resulting in

\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}},t)\mathcal{F}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}},t)\mathfrak{J}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}},t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}\bar{\bar{z}}=\\
\int\bar{\bar{f}}^{0}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0})\mathcal{F}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}(t;\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0}))\mathfrak{J}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}(t;\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0}),t)\mathfrak{H}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}(t;\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0}),t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{6}\bar{\bar{z}}^{0}

(G.4)

by defining the Jacobian

\mathfrak{H}=\det\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0}}

(G.5)

and by making use of
Eq.

4.2
.
Therefore, we must show that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathfrak{H})=0,

(G.6)

where we note that the product
\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathfrak{H}
is the Jacobian of the coordinate transformation from initial gyrocentre coordinates to physical coordinates.

We note that
Eq.

G.1
implies

\dot{\mathfrak{J}}_{s}=-\mathfrak{J}_{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}.

(G.7)

In order to proceed, we make use of (for a proof we refer to
(
Bouchut et al. 2000
, Proposition 1.1)
)

\dot{\mathfrak{H}}=\mathfrak{H}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}.

(G.8)

When combining this result with
Eq.

G.7
we find

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathfrak{H})=\dot{\mathfrak{J}}_{s}\mathfrak{H}+\mathfrak{J}_{s}\dot{\mathfrak{H}}=\dot{\mathfrak{J}}_{s}\mathfrak{H}+\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathfrak{H}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}\bcdot\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{Z}}}}}=\dot{\mathfrak{J}}_{s}\mathfrak{H}-\mathfrak{H}\dot{\mathfrak{J}}_{s}=0.

(G.9)

It follows that

\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathfrak{H}=\mathfrak{J}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0})\mathfrak{H}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0})=\mathfrak{J}_{s}(\bar{\bar{{\bm{z}}}}^{0},t^{0}),

(G.10)

which, upon substitution in
Eq.

G.4
, results in
Eq.

G.2
.

Appendix H
Proof of local energy conservation

(H.1)

whereas the potential energy density (

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\bar{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}+\bar{\bar{\mathcal{U}}})+\nabla\bcdot\left({\bm{E}}\times{\bm{\mathcal{H}}}+\sum_{s}\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\bar{\bar{K}}_{s}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}\right)=0.

(H.3)

Proof H.6
.

A direct computation of the partial derivative of the kinetic energy density (
4.75a
) per species results in

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}_{s}}{\partial t}={}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\mathfrak{J}_{s})\bar{\bar{K}}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}-\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\bar{\bar{\mu}}\langle\!\langle(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{E}}_{1})_{\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\nabla\bcdot\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\bar{\bar{K}}_{s}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}+\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\bar{\bar{\mu}}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\bcdot\nabla(B_{0}+\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle)\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

\displaystyle+m_{s}\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{U}}}_{\shortparallel}\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}-\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\bar{\bar{\mu}}\langle\!\langle(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{E}}_{1})_{\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\nabla\bcdot\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\bar{\bar{K}}_{s}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}+\frac{1}{m_{s}}\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\bar{\bar{\mu}}\left(\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{B}}_{s}^{\star}-{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{1}\right)\bcdot\nabla(B_{0}+\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m_{s}}\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}{\bm{B}}_{s}^{\star}\bcdot\left[q_{s}{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{1}-\bar{\bar{\mu}}\nabla(B_{0}+\langle\!\langle\mathring{B}^{\varsigma}_{1,\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle)\right]\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}-\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\bar{\bar{\mu}}\langle\!\langle(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{E}}_{1})_{\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\nabla\bcdot\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\bar{\bar{K}}_{s}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}+q_{s}\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\dot{\bar{\bar{{\bm{R}}}}}\bcdot{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{1}\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}-\int\bar{\bar{f}}_{s}\bar{\bar{\mu}}\langle\!\langle(\mathring{\nabla}^{\varsigma}\times{\bm{E}}_{1})_{\shortparallel}\rangle\!\rangle\mathfrak{J}_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}},

(H.4)

upon substitution of Faraday’s equation (

).

For computing the partial time derivative of the potential energy density (

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}({\bm{\mathcal{D}}}\bcdot{\bm{E}})=2\frac{\partial{\bm{\mathcal{D}}}}{\partial t}\bcdot{\bm{E}}+\sum_{s}\frac{m_{s}}{B_{0}^{2}}\int\bar{\bar{f}}^{0}_{s}\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}\left[({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})\bcdot\frac{\partial{\bm{E}}}{\partial t}-\left({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\frac{\partial{\bm{B}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right)\bcdot{\bm{E}}\right]\mathfrak{J}_{00,s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

(H.5)

and similarly

446.00386pt}{}^{\mathrm{f}}\bcdot{\bm{E}}.

(H.7)

It remains to be shown that the two remaining terms from
Eqs.

H.5
and
H.6
vanish.
The sum of the two terms is written as

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{m_{s}}{B_{0}^{2}}\int\bar{\bar{f}}^{0}_{s}\Biggl(

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}\left[({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{B}}_{1})\bcdot\frac{\partial{\bm{E}}_{1}}{\partial t}-\left({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\frac{\partial{\bm{B}}_{1}}{\partial t}\right)\bcdot{\bm{E}}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial{\bm{B}}_{1}}{\partial t}\bcdot\left[\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}+\left(\frac{\bar{\bar{\mu}}B_{0}}{m_{s}}-\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}^{2}\right){\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left[\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}{\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times{\bm{E}}_{1,\perp}+\left(\frac{\bar{\bar{\mu}}B_{0}}{m_{s}}-\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}^{2}\right){\bm{B}}_{1,\perp}\right]\bcdot{\bm{B}}_{1}\biggr)\mathfrak{J}_{00,s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\bar{\bar{u}}

(H.8)

upon substitution of the definition of the magnetization and polarization as defined in Eqs. (
4.39
) and (
4.34
), respectively.
Indeed, the sum vanishes and therefore
Eq.

H.2
also holds.

Appendix I
Derivation of the Brizard–Hahm Hamiltonian

The aim is to compute the ZLR approximation of the second-order Hamiltonian as found in
(
Brizard & Hahm 2007
, Eq. (173))
.
Their first- and second-order Hamiltonian are given by

\bar{\bar{H}}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}=q\langle{\psi}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}\rangle,\quad{\psi}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathring{\phi}_{1}-\bar{\bar{u}}_{\shortparallel}\widetilde{A}_{1,\shortparallel}-\bar{\bar{u}}_{\tau}\mathring{A}_{1,\tau}

(I.1a)

and

\bar{\bar{H}}_{2}^{\mathrm{BH}}=-\frac{q}{2}\langle\{{\bar{\bar{S}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}},\widetilde{\psi}{}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}\}_{0}\rangle+\frac{q^{2}}{2m}\left[\langle\lvert\mathring{{\bm{A}}}_{1,\perp}\rvert^{2}\rangle+\langle(\widetilde{A}_{1,\shortparallel})^{2}\rangle\right]+\frac{q}{B_{0}}\langle\mathring{{\bm{A}}}_{1,\perp}\rangle\bcdot({\bm{\hat{b}}}_{0}\times\nabla\langle{\psi}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}\rangle),

(I.1b)

respectively.
The first-order generating function
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}
again satisfies
Eq.

3.48
to zeroth-order in
\varepsilon_{\omega}
.

I.1
ZLR approximation of the Poisson bracket

We use the following shorthand notation for the components of a vector field
{\bm{S}}
in terms of the local coordinates
{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{1},{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{2}

S_{\hat{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{S}}\bcdot{\bm{\hat{e}}}_{i}

(I.2)

as well as the corresponding directional derivative of a scalar function

\partial_{\hat{i}}Q\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\bm{\hat{e}}}_{i}\bcdot\nabla Q.

(I.3)

Provided with
\bar{\bar{S}}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}
, we want to approximate the leading order term in
\varepsilon_{\perp}
of the gyro-averaged guiding-centre Poisson bracket (
2.5
) while neglecting
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\omega})
and
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{B})
terms.
This results in

\langle\{{\bar{\bar{S}}}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}},\widetilde{\psi}{}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}}\}_{0}\rangle=\underbrace{\frac{q}{B_{0}}\left\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bar{\mu}}}(\widetilde{\psi}{}_{1}^{\mathrm{BH}})^{2}\right\rangle}_{\hbox to10.27pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.1355pt\lower-5.1355pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

is the only

B.2
.
